                                                                 The local entropy along Ricci flow
                                                                        —Part B: the pseudo-locality theorems

                                                                                         Bing Wang




arXiv:2010.09981v1 [math.DG] 20 Oct 2020
                                                                                           Abstract
                                                     We localize the entropy functionals of G. Perelman and generalize his no-local-collapsing
                                                 theorem and pseudo-locality theorem. Our generalization is technically inspired by further
                                                 development of Li-Yau estimates along the Ricci flow. It has various applications, including to
                                                 show the continuous dependence of the Ricci flow with respect to the initial metric in Gromov-
                                                 Hausdorff topology with Ricci curvature bounded below, and to show the compactness of
                                                 the moduli of Kähler manifolds with bounded scalar curvature and a rough locally almost
                                                 Euclidean condition.

                                           5 Distance distortion and topological stability                                                         42

                                           8 The pseudo-locality theorem in the Kähler Ricci flow                                                 83

                                           9 Further Questions                                                                                     101


                                           1 Introduction

                                           In the celebrated work [83], Perelman introduced his famous functionals µ and ν, which are mono-
                                           tone along the Ricci flow. These functionals can be naturally localized(cf. [87], [104] and [99]).

                                                                                               1
Inspired by further developing the seminal estimates of Li-Yau [73], the author [99] investigated
the evolution of localized functionals along the Ricci flow and derived effective monotonicity for-
mulas for them. Based on these formulas, the author improved the no-local-collapsing theorem of
Perelman to the following version.

Theorem 1.1 (Improved version of no-local-collapsing, cf. Theorem 1.1 of [99]). For every
A > 1 there exists κ = κ(m, A) > 0 with the following property. Suppose {(M m , g(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ r02 } is
a Ricci flow solution such that

     r02 |Rm|(x, t) ≤ m−1 ,   ∀ x ∈ Bg(0) (x0 , r0 ), 0 ≤ t ≤ r02 ;          r0−m Bg(0) (x0 , r0 ) dv      ≥ A−1 .   (1.1)

whenever A−1 r02 ≤ t0 ≤ r02 , 0 < r ≤ r0 , and Bg(t0 ) (x, r) ⊂ Bg(t0 ) (x0 , Ar0 ) is a geodesic ball satisfying
r2 R(·, t0 ) ≤ 1.

   Theorem 1.1 improves the no-local-collapsing theorem of Perelman(cf. Theorem 8.2 of [83]).
Here we replace the requirement of space-time condition by a time-slice condition around (x, t).
Namely, we only need R(·, t) ≤ r−2 in the ball Bg(t) (x, r) to conclude non-collapsing of Bg(t) (x, r)
whenever (x, t) is not very far away from (x0 , 0). However, we still require space-time condition
(1.1) around the base (x0 , 0). The condition (1.1) is not optimal and can be replaced by Ricci
curvature bound. In fact, in Theorem 7.2 of [99], we assumed a much weaker condition
                                                                             √
                        t|Rc|(x, t) < (m − 1)A,         ∀ x ∈ Bg(t) (x,          t),   0<t<T                         (1.3)

to play the same role as (1.1). Although the condition (1.1) can be obtained in the Kähler setting
under many circumstances, it looks artificial in the Riemannian setting. A condition depending
only on the initial metric g(0) should be more natural. This leads us to develop the following
pseudo-locality theorem, which improves the known pseudo-locality theorems(cf. Theorem 10.1
and Corollary 10.3 of Perelman [83], also Proposition 3.1 and Theorem 3.1 of Tian-Wang [95]).

       inequality
              √  (1.4) can be interpreted as a new “almost Euclidean” condition for the domain
Bg(0) x0 , δ−1 T equipped with initial metric g(0). The functionals µ and ν are the localized
functionals of Perelman(cf.(2.3)-(2.10) for precise
                                                    definitions).
                                                        √         The left hand side of (1.4) is com-
pletely determined by the geometry of Bg(0) x0 , δ−1 T with initial metric g(0). In particular,
                                             √ 
(1.4) holds whenever the ball Bg(0) x0 , δ−1 T satisfies one of the following conditions: either
the ball has small scalar curvature lower bound and almost Euclidean isoperimetric constant, or
the ball has small Ricci curvature lower bound and almost Euclidean volume ratio. Therefore,
it is not hard to see(cf. Corollary 4.11 and Corollary 4.12) that (1.4) is weaker than the condi-
tions in the original version and in Tian-Wang’s version of Perelman’s pseudo-locality theorem.
While it is known now that almost non-negative Ricci curvature and almost Euclidean volume
ratio together imply almost Euclidean isoperimetric constant [15], the proof is complicated and
involves the deep theory of RCD spaces and optimal transportation. Therefore, it is still worth-
while to provide a more self-contained and elementary proof to unify the pseudo-locality theorems
of Perelman [83] and Tian-Wang [95]. More importantly, the local entropy has an obvious advan-
tage over the isoperimetric constant: the local entropy is almost monotone along the Ricci flow(cf.
Theorem 2.10), however, the monotonicity of isoperimetric constant along the Ricci flow is not
clear. This advantage makes Theorem 1.2 an effective tool to estimate the lifespan of the Ricci
flow solution(cf. Corollary 5.5 and Corollary 5.6).
                                              √
    In Theorem 1.2, if we assume r0 = δ−1 T and the ball Bg(0) (x0 , r0 ) is “almost Euclidean”,
then we obtain curvature and volume estimate around base point x0 on the time period (0, (δr0 )2 ],
as illustrated in Figure 1. Consequently, the condition (1.3) is naturally implied by the almost
Euclidean initial condition. If one allows time-shifting and rescaling, then even the much stronger
condition (1.1) can be obtained by a rough almost Euclidean initial condition like (1.4). There-
fore, even in the Riemannian setting, the condition in Theorem 1.1 can be deduced from local
information of the initial metric.
   Theorem 1.2 can be regarded as a bridge between the Ricci flow theory and the structure theory
of Cheeger-Colding concerning the Riemannian manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded below.

                                                        3
Both theories are of fundamental importance and have essentially changed the landscape of Rie-
mannian geometry in last two decades. In the resolution of the Hamilton-Tian conjecture on Fano
Kähler Ricci flow(cf. [33], [34] and [35]) by Chen and the author, it is a key point to further de-
velop the Cheeger-Colding theory to obtain a compactness theorem for non-collapsed Calabi-Yau
spaces with mild singularities. Such a compactness result then played a crucial role in showing the
convergence of the Kähler Ricci flow, via the Bergman Kernel and Perelman’s reduced volume.
The compactness theory for the moduli space of gradient shrinking solitons-the singularity model
spaces for Ricci flows-has also been developed in a series of works [75], [63] and [78]. Along
the same line, we shall in this paper discuss further applications of the Cheeger-Colding theory
in the study of Ricci flows. On the other hand, we shall also see how one can exploit Ricci flow
techniques to improve the understanding of the Cheeger-Colding theory. The following theorem
directly relates the Ricci flow to the Cheeger-Colding theory.

Theorem 1.3 (Continuous dependence on initial data in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology).
Suppose that N = {(N m , h(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ T } is a (unnormalized, volume normalized, or c-normalized)
Ricci flow solution initiated from h(0) = h, a smooth Riemannian metric on a closed manifold N m .
For each ǫ small, there exists δ = δ(N, ǫ) with the following properties.
   Suppose (M m , g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying

                          Rc > −(m − 1)ǫ −1 ,       dGH {(M, g), (N, h)} < δ.                  (1.9)

Then the (unnormalized, volume normalized, or c-normalized) Ricci flow initiated from (M, g)
exists on [0, T ]. Furthermore, there exists a family of diffeomorphisms {Φt : N → M, ǫ ≤ t ≤ T }
such that
                                 sup Φ∗t g(t) − h(t) C[ǫ−1 ] (N,h(t)) < ǫ.                (1.10)
                               t∈[ǫ,T ]




   As in [99], we call a solution of the following equation as normalized Ricci flow:

                                          ∂
                                             g = −2 {Rc + λ(t)g} .                           (1.11)
                                          ∂t
             >
If λ(t) = m1 M Rdv, then the volume is fixed and the flow is called volume normalized. If λ(t) = c,
then the flow is called c-normalized. Theorem 1.3 claims that the Ricci flow depends on the initial
data continuously in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology, when the Ricci curvature is bounded from
below. The diffeomorphism Φt : N → M in Theorem 1.3 can be constructed explicitly. Conse-
quently, Theorem 1.3 implies that if Mi converges to N in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology with
Ricci bounded below, then Mi is diffeomorphic to N for large i. Therefore, we provide an al-
ternative approach to construct diffeomorphisms, independent of the Reifenberg method, as done
in [18]. In the presence of the local isometry group actions, the diffeomorphisms constructed from
the Ricci flows seem natural and respect the group actions. For further discussions, see [66], [67]
and [64]. The conditions in Theorem 1.3 are not optimal and could be modified in various applica-
tions. In [65], we will develop a version of Theorem 1.3 for certain complete non-compact initial
data, and use this to show that certain torus bundles with almost non-negative Ricci curvature are
trivial.

                                                     4
    Theorem 1.3 implies the commutativity of taking Gromov-Hausdorff limit and running the
Ricci flow, under the condition that the initial Ricci curvatures are uniformly bounded below.
The proof of Theorem 1.3 relies heavily on the improved estimates (1.5), (1.6) and (1.7) in Theo-
rem 1.2. Note that the application of pseudo-locality type theorems are heavily studied in the Ricci
flow literature(cf. [16], [91], [3], [71], [82], [72], [43], [77], etc.). Typically, the other (than Perel-
man’s) pseudo-locality type theorems rely on stronger curvature or dimensional assumptions(e.g.
BK ≥ −C, PIC1 ≥ −C, dim ≤ 3, etc.) and the analysis of ancient solutions with non-negativity
of the strong curvature assumptions, which are preserved by the Ricci flow. A key difference in
Theorem 1.3 is that the non-negativity of Ricci curvature is not preserved in dimension higher than
three(cf. [6], [81]).
    Theorem 1.3 can be used to show the global convergence of Ricci flows near(in the Gromov-
Hausdorff topology)stable Einstein manifolds(cf. Theorem 7.2). Although the Einstein constant
we consider could be negative, zero or positive, as discussed in Section 7, we shall focus only
on the case of positive Einstein constant in this introduction. Among other things, we have the
following global convergence theorem for normalized Ricci flows.
Theorem 1.4 (Stability of normalized Ricci flow near sphere). For each m ≥ 3, there is a
constant δ0 = δ0 (m) with the following property. Suppose (M m , g) is a Riemannian manifold
satisfying                        
                                   Rc ≥ (m − 1)(1 − δ),
                                  
                                  
                                                                                     (1.12)
                                   |M|dvg = (m + 1)ωm+1 ,
                                  

where ωm+1 is the volume of unit ball in Rm+1 , and δ ∈ (0, δ0 ). Then the normalized Ricci flow
initiated from (M m , g) exists immortally and converges to a round metric g∞ exponentially fast.
The limit metric (M, g∞ ) has constant sectional curvature 1.
   Furthermore, for each pair of points x, y ∈ M satisfying dg (x, y) ≤ 1, the following distance
bi-Hölder estimate holds:
                                    1+ψ                          1−ψ
                               e−ψ dg (x, y) ≤ dg∞ (x, y) ≤ eψ dg (x, y),                           (1.13)
where ψ = ψ(δ|m) such that lim ψ(δ|m) = 0.
                              δ→0

    Theorem 1.4 corresponds to certain rigidity phenomenon. If δ = 0 in (1.12), the proof of
Bishop-Gromov volume comparison implies that (M, g) is isometric to the round sphere (S m , ground )
of sectional curvature 1. Consequently, the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (M, g) is static,
which means g∞ = g. Therefore, Theorem 1.4 can be regarded as an effective version of the afore-
mentioned rigidity phenomenon. In Kähler geometry, there is a similar rigidity phenomenon with
the model space (CPn , gFS ), where n = m2 is the complex dimension. Note that the Ricci curvature
of (CPn , gFS ) is 2(n + 1). In the same vein, replacing (S m , ground ) by (CPn , gFS ), one can expect a
Kähler version of Theorem 1.4, which is realized by the following theorem.
Theorem 1.5 (Stability of normalized Ricci flow near projective space). For each integer n ≥ 1,
there exists a constant δ0 = δ0 (n) with the following properties.
    Suppose (M n , g, J) is a Fano manifold whose metric form is proportional to c1 (M, J) and sat-
isfies                               
                                      Rc ≥ 2(n + 1)(1 − δ),
                                     
                                     
                                                                                            (1.14)
                                      |M|dvg = Ωn ,
                                     


                                                    5
where Ωn is the volume of (CPn , gFS ), and δ ∈ (0, δ0 ). Then the normalized Ricci flow initiated
from (M, g) exists immortally and converges smoothly(without further diffeomorphisms) to a metric
g∞ such that (M, g∞ , J) is biholomorphic-isometric to (CPn , gFS , JFS ). Namely, there exists a
diffeomorphism Φ : CPn → M such that Φ∗ g∞ = gFS and Φ∗ (J) = JFS . Furthermore, for each
pair of points x, y ∈ M satisfying dg (x, y) ≤ 1, the distance bi-Hölder estimate (1.13) holds.

    Note that the bi-Hölder distance estimate in Theorem 1.5 implies the following conclusion(cf.
Corollary 7.10). Under the condition Rc ≥ 2(n + 1) and g is in a Kähler class proportional to
c1 (M, J), the Kähler manifold (M, g, J) is close to (CPn , gFS , JFS ) in Gromov-Hausdorff topology
if and only if the volume of (M, g, J) is close to Ωn . This result is the Kähler version of the
theorems of Colding(cf. [38] and [39]). Theorem 1.4 also has a non-compact version. In fact, from
Bishop-Gromov volume comparison, it is clear that a complete Riemannian manifold (M m , g) with
non-negative Ricci curvature and asymptotic volume ratio AVR(M, g) = 1 must be isometric to
Euclidean space (Rm , gE ). Here

                                AVR(M, g) ≔ lim ω−1 −m
                                                 m r |B(x0 , r)|
                                                      r→∞

for some x0 ∈ M. It is not hard to see that the above definition is independent of the choice of base
point x0 ∈ M. The Ricci flow started from (M, g) is then static and thus is always isometric to the
Euclidean space. This rigidity phenomenon is the non-compact counterpart of the one underlying
Theorem 1.4. Therefore, the “almost rigidity” shall be some effective estimate of the Ricci flow,
which is realized by the following theorem.

Theorem 1.6 (Almost Euclidean immortal Ricci flow). For each m ≥ 3, there is a constant
δ0 = δ0 (m) with the following property. Suppose (M m , g) is a complete non-compact Riemannian
manifold satisfying                  

                                     

for some δ ∈ (0, δ0 ). Then there exists an immortal Ricci flow solution initiated from (M m , g)
satisfying

                                         − 21                 −1

for every x ∈ M and t > 0. Furthermore, for each pair of points x, y ∈ M and t > 0, the following
distance distortion estimate holds:
                                                     (             √        )

   If we set δ = 0 in Theorem 1.6, then we return to the rigidity phenomenon discussed before.
Theorem 1.6 has a topological application. Based on the curvature estimate (1.16), the injectivity
radius estimate (1.17) and the distance distortion estimate (1.18), we can construct(cf. Theo-
rem 5.7) a canonical diffeomorphism from M to the Euclidean space Rm . Again, the existence of


                                                        6
the diffeomorphism from M to Rm was already known by Cheeger-Colding [18], via the Reifen-
berg method. We expect our construction here to provide new perspectives on the Reifenberg
method, which has far-reaching influences in geometric analysis and is still quickly evolving right
now(cf. [21] and [60]).
    Comparing Theorem 1.6 with its compact version Theorem 1.4, one immediately realizes that
we miss the discussion of exponential convergence of the flow in Theorem 1.6. By the curva-
ture and injectivity radius estimates (1.16) and (1.17), we have the convergence of (M, x0 , g(ti ))
to Euclidean space (Rm , 0, gE ) in the pointed-Cheeger-Gromov topology, by taking subsequence
if necessary. However, in Cheeger-Gromov convergence, one needs further modifications by dif-
feomorphisms, which make the convergence weaker than the convergence of the metric tensors in
fixed coordinates, as achieved by Theorem 1.4. Actually, in fixed coordinate charts, the immortal
Ricci flow in Theorem 1.6 must diverge unless it is the static Euclidean flow. Detailed analysis on
the immortal Ricci flow(cf. Proposition 5.14) in Theorem 1.6 shows that

                                      lim inf R(x, t)t ≥ ψ(δ|m),                                (1.19)
                                        t→∞

and the above limit is independent of the choice of x ∈ M. Therefore, dvg(t) will degenerate as
t → ∞. The scalar curvature lower bound in (1.19) is basically caused by the fact that the tangent
cone of (M, g) at infinity is not isometric to (Rm , gE ). If we relax the non-negative Ricci curvature
condition to non-negative scalar curvature condition, then it is possible that the tangent cone of
(M, g) at infinity is isometric to (Rm , gE ) and (M, g) itself is non-flat. For example, when (M, g)
is an AE manifold with non-negative scalar curvature, the almost non-negativity of ν-functional
can be combined with the work of Y. Li [76] to obtain the global existence and convergence of the
flow in fixed coordinate charts.
    The failure of the convergence of the immortal flow in Theorem 1.6 suggests that the metric dis-
tortion estimate (1.18) is optimal. Note that if the right hand side of (1.18) is uniformly bounded,
then we have uniform metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence along the flow, which cannot hold by our
previous discussion. In fact, even on closed Kähler manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded from
below and almost Euclidean initial condition on a fixed scale, we explicitly construct examples
which show the failure of the bi-Lipschitz equivalence. For more details, see Proposition 7.13
and related discussion in Section 7. Therefore, the single condition of the Ricci curvature lower
bound is too weak for the metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence to hold along the Ricci flow. Inspired by
the fundamental works on complex Monge-Ampère equation by Yau [101] and Chen-Cheng [25],
we are able to show that the metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence holds under the almost Euclidean
condition, if we replace the lower bound of the Ricci curvature by the two-sided bound of the
scalar curvature. Based on the metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence, we can essentially improve the
pseudo-locality theorem in the Kähler setting.

Theorem 1.7 (Pseudo-locality in the Kähler Ricci flow). For each α ∈ (0, 1) and Q > 1, there
is a constant ǫ = ǫ(n, α, Q) with the following properties.
   Suppose {(M n , g(t), J), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1} is a Ricci flow solution, x0 ∈ M and r ∈ (0, 1). Suppose
                                
                                 ν̄(Bg(0) (x0 , r), g(0), r2 ) ≥ −ǫ;
                                
                                
                                                                                               (1.20)
                                 |R(x, 0)| ≤ r−2 , ∀ x ∈ Bg(0) (x0 , r).
                                


                                                  7
Then there is a biholomorphic map Φ : Bg(0) (x0 , ǫr) → U ⊂ Cn such that

                           B(0, Q−1 ) ⊂ U ⊂ B(0, Q);                                              (1.21)
                                −2                                     2 2
                            (ǫr) Φ∗ g(t) C1,α (U) < Q, ∀ t ∈ [0, ǫ r ];                           (1.22)
                           |R|(x, t) < Q(ǫr)−2 ,    ∀ x ∈ U, t ∈ [0, ǫ 2 r2 ].                    (1.23)


   Note that the estimate (1.22) implies that the metric g(t) and g(0) are uniformly equivalent in
the C 1,α -topology nearby (x0 , 0). In particular, we have the bi-Lipschitz equivalence
                1
                  g(y, 0) ≤ g(y, t) ≤ Qg(y, 0),     ∀ y ∈ Bg(0) (x0 , ǫr),    t ∈ [0, ǫ 2 r2 ].
                Q
Theorem 1.7 provides us a method to construct uniform holomorphic charts from a very weak
assumption (1.20). Note that the lower bound of ν̄ in (1.20) can be achieved if the underlying
local isoperimetric constant is very close to the Euclidean one. Under such a condition and
Ricci curvature lower bound, similar construction of holomorphic charts was developed in Liu-
Székelyhidi [79]. The key improvements of Theorem 1.7 are the estimates (1.22) and (1.23),
which imply the following theorem.

Theorem 1.8 (Preservation of two-sided scalar curvature bound). Suppose (Min , gi , Ji ) is a
sequence of Kähler manifolds with uniformly bounded scalar curvatures and diameters. Suppose
this sequence converges to a smooth Riemannian manifold (N, g) in C 0 -topology in the sense that
there exist diffeomorphisms fi : N → Mi such that

                                      lim fi∗ gi − g C0 (g) = 0.                                  (1.24)
                                      i→∞

Then N admits a natural smooth Kähler structure J. Furthermore, both the upper and lower
bounds of the scalar curvature are continuous under the convergence. Namely, if the scalar cur-
vature satisfies σa,i ≤ Rgi ≤ σb,i for each i, then we have

                                            σa ≤ R g ≤ σb ,                                       (1.25)

where
                              σa ≔ lim sup σa,i ,       σb ≔ lim inf σb,i .                       (1.26)
                                      i→∞                       i→∞

If σa = σb , then (N, g, J) is a smooth cscK(constant scalar curvature Kähler) manifold.

    The preservation of scalar curvature lower bound along convergence has been well-studied, for
example, in Lott [80], Honda [58], etc. See also Sormani [92] for a survey. Without any cur-
vature assumption, the preservation of scalar curvature lower bound under C 0 -convergence was
first proved by Gromov [49] in the Riemannian setting. An alternative proof was later provided
by Bamler [2], using the Ricci flow. Theorem 1.8 indicates that in the Kähler setting, both the
lower and upper bounds of the scalar curvature are preserved under the C 0 -convergence. This is
reasonable since the scalar curvature in Kähler geometry carries much more information than that

                                                    8
in Riemannian geometry. Calabi [12] initiated the study of cscK metrics and introduced the Calabi
flow to search for such metrics. In [45], Donaldson pointed out that in the space of all complex
structures compatible with a given metric form, the scalar curvature function can be regarded as
a moment map. The famous Yau-Tian-Donaldson conjecture predicts that the existence of cscK
metrics is equivalent to the stability of complex structures. For more information in this direction,
see the most recent breakthrough of Chen-Cheng(cf. [25], [26], [27] and the references therein).
We hope Theorem 1.8 could shed some light on the problem of finding cscK metrics.

   We now briefly describe how to prove the aforementioned theorems.

Outline proof of Theorem 1.2: We shall basically follow the ideas of Perelman [83], with some
technical differences. One key difference is the size of the domain where the√curvature and
volume estimates hold. Similar to [95], the balls we concern here are Bg(t) (x0 , t), rather than
Bg(t) (x0 , ǫr0 ) as in Theorem 10.1 of Perelman [83]. Such difference is crucial for the subsequent
blowup argument under the condition (1.4) and is very helpful to simplify the steps of the proof(cf.
Remark 4.2).
   We argue by contradiction. We first prove (1.5) for α = A sufficiently large. By continuity, (1.5)
holds for very small t. Let t0 be the first time that they start to fail. So there exists a point (y0 , t0 )
                                            √
with |Rm|(y0 , t0 ) = α and y0 ∈ Bg(t) (x0 , t0 ). Via an auxiliary function, we then obtain a local(in
                                                                                                      √
the space-time) maximum curvature point (z0 , s0 ) such that s0 ∈ [0.5t0 , t0 ], z0 ∈ Bg(s0 ) (x0 , 10 s0 ).
According to the local functional monotonicity, the almost Euclidean µ-functional at time t = 0
implies an almost Euclidean ν-functional value of the proper neighborhood containing (z0 , s0 ).
After a proper rescaling, the point (z0 , s0 ) has |Rm| being equal to 1 and nearby curvature all
bounded by 2. By scaling invariance of ν, the neighborhood of (z0 , s0 ) has ν value very close to
0 also. Recall that the lower bound of ν implies the lower bound of volume ratio. Therefore we
can take a limit of the neighborhoods around (z0 , s0 ) and obtain a complete Riemannian manifold
with ν ≥ 0, which must be Euclidean. However, the limit cannot be flat since its base point has
curvature 1. Contradiction.
                                                                        1
    We then proceed to prove (1.5) for arbitrary small α ∈ (0, 100m        ). Note that µ is monotone for the
containment relationship. Applying the first step       √ to all balls inside the given initial ball, for each
point (x, t) satisfying dg(t) (x, ∂Bg(0) (x0 , r0 )) > A t, we obtain rough curvature estimates. Another
blowup argument implies
                      √       that the curvature bound t|Rm|(·, t) < A can be improved to t|Rm|(·, t) < α
when dg(t) (x, x0 ) < t. Since the blowup limit is Euclidean, the argument also implies the volume
estimate (1.6) and the injectivity radius estimate (1.7).
   For full details of the proof, see Section 3 and Section 4.                                              

Outline proof of Theorem 1.3: By the volume continuity of Colding [40], we know that for large
i, there is a uniform scale r0 such that each geodesic ball under this scale has almost Euclidean
volume ratio. Then one can apply Theorem 1.2 to obtain a uniform existence time of the flow, say
on [0, 1]. Fix a small positive number ξ0 . By the curvature estimate (1.5) and volume estimate
(1.6), it is clear that each unit geodesic ball under ξ0−1 g(ξ0 ) is very close to the unit ball in the
Euclidean space. The curvature condition also forces the distance to be almost expanding along
the flow. Together with the almost non-negativity of the scalar curvature, we see that each unit ball
of the metric ξ0−1 g(0) has almost Euclidean volume ratio and almost non-negative Ricci curvature.

                                                      9
These bounds imply a local almost Einstein condition in the sense of Tian-Wang [95]. Following
the argument in Section 4 of [95], we obtain a distance distortion estimate(cf. Chen-Rong-Xu [23]
also) between ξ0−1 g(ξ0 ) and ξ0−1 g(0). Note that (M, ξ0−1 g(0)) converges to (N, ξ0−1 h(0)). So we
have Gromov-Hausdorff approximation F̂ : (M, ξ0−1 g(0)) → (N, ξ0−1 h(0)) and Ĝ : (N, ξ0−1 h(0)) →
(M, ξ0−1 g(0)). By the metric distortion estimate on these two Ricci flows, they induce Gromov-
Hausdorff approximations F̃ and G̃ between (M, ξ0−1 g(ξ0 )) and (N, ξ0−1 h(ξ0 )), both of which are
almost flat and have almost Euclidean volume ratio on scale 10. Then standard center of mass
argument
           can be applied to deform G̃ to G, which is a diffeomorphism from N to M such that
G ξ0 g(ξ0 ) is very close to ξ0−1 h(ξ0 ) in the C 0 -sense. In particular, G∗ (g(ξ0 )) is close to h(ξ0 )
  ∗    −1

in C 0 -norm. Since the flow on N is smooth, we know that h(ξ0 ) has to be very close to h(0) in
C 0 -sense also. Therefore, we obtain the C 0 -closeness between g(ξ0 ) and h(0). Then we run the
Ricci-DeTurck flow from g(ξ0 ) and h(0) respectively, it follows from the strict parabolicity of the
Ricci-DeTurck flow that such C 0 -closeness is preserved until time T , up to a multiplicative con-
stant. Finally, we construct diffeomorphisms which pull back the Ricci-Deturk flows to the Ricci
flows. This construction is standard in the proof of the uniqueness of the Ricci flow solution. The
proximity of Ricci-DeTurck flows then implies the closeness of the Ricci flows in C 0 -sense. The
high-order derivatives approximation of the Ricci flow then follows from Shi’s estimate(cf. [89]).
   The complete details of the proof can be found in Section 6.                                        

Outline proof of Theorem 1.4: It follows from the work of Colding(cf. [38] and [39]) that the
condition (1.12) implies that dGH ((M m , g), (S m , ground )) is very small. Applying Theorem 1.2
nearby the static normalized Ricci flow initiated from (S m , ground ), we see that g(1) is very close
to ground in the C 5 -topology. In particular, the curvature operator of g(1) is sufficiently pinched.
Therefore, one can apply Hamilton’s work(cf. [51], [52] if dim ≤ 4) and Huisken’s work(cf. [59]
if dim ≥ 5) to obtain the exponential convergence of the normalized Ricci flow initiated from
g(1). Alternatively, one can apply other related convergence results(cf. Böhm-Wilking [7] and
Brendle-Schoen [9], etc.) here to draw the same conclusion.
   The same argument works for every g(ξ) for each fixed ξ. Then the metric g(ξ) and g(∞) are
bi-Lipschitz with bi-Lipschitz constant almost 1. The metric distortion estimate (1.13) is basically
induced by the distortion between g(ξ) and g = g(0), which can be estimated by the pseudo-locality
theorem and the local almost Einstein condition implied by the given condition.
   The detailed proof is contained in Section 7.                                                       

Outline proof of Theorem 1.5: Note that condition (1.14) implies that (M n , J) is biholomorphic to
CPn (cf. Zhang [103]). As the metric class is proportional to 2πc1 (M, J), the volume condition
forces g and gFS to locate in the same Kähler class. In the current situation, the normalized
Ricci flow initiated from g is the standard Fano Kähler Ricci flow and thus it exists immortally(cf.
Cao [14]). The difficulty here is to obtain the metric distortion estimate (1.13). Compared with the
Riemannian version Theorem 1.4, an equivalence theorem similar to Colding’s(cf. [38] and [39])
is absent here. We shall apply the techniques in Tian-Wang [95] and Chen-Wang [34] to overcome
this difficulty. In fact, by an improvement of Perelman’s estimate(cf. Jiang [68]), we can apply
Chen-Wang’s compactness theorem for the flow space time M × [0.5, 2]. As δ → 0, we know this
flow space-time converges to a static(possibly singular) Einstein space-time, which can only be
the standard Fubini-Study space-time on CPn by the partial-C 0 -estimate and the stability condition

                                                   10
satisfied by CPn (cf. [28], [29], [30] and [31]). Consequently, the degeneration must be smooth.
Therefore, if δ is sufficiently small, the flow has positive bisectional curvature on M × [0.5, 2].
Then the global exponential convergence of the flow follows from Chen-Tian [32](See also Phong-
Song-Weinkove [85]). As δ → 0, we can obtain an almost Einstein sequence in the sense that
R 1R
 0 M
        |Rc − 2(n + 1)g|dvdt → 0. At each time t0 ∈ (0, 1), we know from the previous argument that
g(t0 ) converges smoothly to gFS . For time t = 0, we know g(0) converges to a possibly singular
Einstein manifold by Tian-Wang [95]. A gap argument then shows that the limit of g(0) must
be smooth and be isometric to gFS also. This forces the almost Euclidean condition (1.8) to be
satisfied on a fixed scale. Then we can apply pseudo-locality, i.e., Theorem 1.2, and the almost
Einstein condition to obtain the distance distortion estimate, in the spirit of section 4 of [95].
   The detailed proof can be found in Section 7.                                                    

Outline proof of Theorem 1.6: This basically follows from the application of the pseudo-locality
theorem and the Hochard’s construction [57]. Similar results were already obtained by F. He [56].
The Ricci flow is known to have a solution existing for a fixed short period of time, say on [0, 1].
The key point now is to extend this solution to an immortal solution. We realize this by taking
advantage of our version of pseudo-locality in terms of ν, condition (1.8). Note that by our local
functional monotonicity proved in [99], it is not hard to see that ν(M, g(t)) is non-decreasing.
Therefore, at time t = 1, we still have ν(M, g(1)) ≥ −ǫ, which guarantees the flow to exist for
another unit time peoriod. Repeating such steps, we obtain an immortal Ricci flow solution.
Then the curvature estimate (1.16) and the injectivity radius estimate (1.17) follow naturally from
(1.5) and (1.7). We remark that the constants in (1.16) and (1.17) being sufficiently small is very
important for our construction of the canonical diffeomorphism from M to Rm .
   The readers are referred to Section 5 for the complete proof and related further discussions.    

Outline proof of Theorem 1.7: The scalar curvature bound basically follows from the maximum
principle and the evolution equation of R and R + |∇ϕ̇|2 where ϕ̇ is the local Ricci potential. The
C 1,α -estimate of metrics follows from the scalar curvature bound together with the metric uniform
bi-Lipschitz bound. The key difficulty is to show the uniform metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence
in a short time period near the center of the ball. This equivalence is established through the
combination of distance distortion estimates originated from Tian-Wang [95] and the regularity
estimates for Kähler metrics with bounded scalar curvature in Chen-Cheng [25]. We shall focus
on this key difficulty in this outline.
    By the pseudo-locality theorem in the Riemannian setting, one should be able to construct uni-
form holomorphic charts(cf. [79]) for t small. We need to show at this time the metric g(t) and g(0)
are uniformly bi-Lipschitz near the center part. The proof follows from a contradiction argument.
Suppose not, we can choose t0 to be the first time such that the metric g(t0 ) is (1 + ξ)-bi-Lipschitz
equivalent to g(0) at (x0 , t0 ) for some fixed small ξ. Then we rescale to let t0 = 1 and let ǫ → 0.
The blowup limit of the initial balls will be a Euclidean space, in light of the assumed metric equiv-
alence, and the elliptic PDE estimates for bounded scalar curvature metric in a given holomorphic
coordinate chart. The elliptic PDE estimates together with the metric distortion induced from the
almost Einstein condition forces the identity map (M, g(1)) 7→ (M, g(0)) to converge to an isome-
try. On the one hand, the metric g(1) converges in smooth topology to δkl¯, the standard Euclidean
metric on Cn . On the other hand, the metric g(0) converges in C 1,α -topology to a limit metric ĝ

                                                 11
on Cn which satisfies det ĝ = 1 on Cn . The Evans-Krylov estimate for complex Monge-Ampère
equation then implies that ĝ must be a constant matrix valued function on Cn . Applying the metric
distortion estimate again, one obtains that ĝ must also be δkl¯. Since the convergence topology is
C 1,α , we obtain that the equivalence constant between g(x0 , 0) and g(x0 , 1) can be arbitrarily close
to 1, which contradicts the assumption.
   The complete proof is written in more detail in Section 8.                                            

Outline proof of Theorem 1.8: The C 0 -convergence implies that under a fixed scale r0 , all the
manifolds we consider have almost Euclidean isoperimetric constants. In particular, we are able to
apply the pseudo-locality theorem to run the Kähler Ricci flow initiated from (Mi , gi ) for a fixed pe-
riod of time [0, ξ0 ]. Applying Theorem 1.7, we know (Mi , gi (ξ0 ), Ji ) converges to a smooth Kähler
manifold (M∞ , g∞ (ξ0 ), J∞ ) and gi converges in C 1,α -topology to a limit metric g∞ (0), which is at
least C 1,α . The Riemannian manifold (M∞ , g∞ (0)) is isometric to the smooth Riemannian mani-
fold (N, g). Since the holomorphic charts are automatically harmonic and the metrics have the best
regularity under the harmonic charts, we know that (M∞ , g∞ (0), J∞ ) is a smooth Kähler manifold.
There is a smooth diffeomorphism F : N → M∞ such that J = F ∗ J∞ , g = F ∗ g∞ (0).
   For each t ∈ [0, ξ0 ], we show by the maximum principle that σa − Ctβ ≤ Ri ≤ σb + Ctβ for
some uniform constant C and β ∈ (0, 1). Then the scalar curvature bounds naturally follow from
the commutativity of taking C 0 -limits with running the Kähler Ricci flow, and the aforementioned
scalar curvature bounds along the evolution.
   For further details of the proof and other related results, see Section 8.                            

   Some notations frequently used in this paper are listed below with pointers to their definitions.

List of important notations

    • µ, ν: local functionals with scalar curvature. Defined in (2.9).

    • µ̄, ν̄: local functionals without scalar curvature. Defined in (2.10).

    • erǫ0 , Irǫ0 , vrǫ0 : entropy radius, isoperimetric radius and volume radius. Defined in Defini-
      tion 5.10.

    • I: isoperimetric constant. First appears in Lemma 2.9.

    • m: the default real dimension of a Riemannian manifold (M, g).

    • ωm : the volume of standard unit ball in Rm . Defined in Theorem 1.4.

    • αm : a small dimensional constant defined in (2.1).

    • n: the default complex dimension of a Kähler manifold (M, g, J).

    • Ωn : the volume of (CPn , gFS ). Defined in Theorem 1.4.

    • ψ(ǫ|m, γ1 , · · · , γk ): a small constant depending on parameters m, γ1 , · · · , γk and ǫ such that
      lim ψ(ǫ|m, γ1 , · · · , γk ) = 0. First appears in Theorem 1.4.
      ǫ→0


                                                    12
   • f+ : max { f, 0}. Similar notation is defined for other functions. Defined in Theorem 5.1.
                 
   • log+ : max log, 0 . Defined in Proposition 2.7.

   • : Heat operator ∂t − ∆. Defined in Theorem 5.1.

   • cscK: constant scalar curvature Kähler. Defined in Theorem 1.8.

   • McscK (n, r0 , ǭ, σ, D): moduli of cscK manifolds with proper bounds. Defined in Theo-
     rem 3.9.

    This paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we list some elementary estimates of the Ricci
flow and many properties of the local functionals. Most of these properties were already proved
in [99] except for some minor improvements. In Section 3, we prove several rigidity theorems as
the preparation for the pseudo-locality theorem. In particular, we generalize Anderson’s gap the-
orem for Ricci-flat metrics to gap theorems for various critical metrics, including Einstein metrics
and cscK metrics, in terms of the ν-functionals. In Section 4, we prove Theorem 1.2 and discuss
its relationship with the known pseudo-locality theorems. In Section 5, we show distance distor-
tion estimate and topological stability, based on the almost-Einstein techniques in [95]. We also
discuss the asymptotic behavior of the immortal Ricci flow in Theorem 1.6 in detail. In Section 6,
we prove the continuous dependence of the Ricci flow with respect to the initial metric in Gromov-
Hausdorff topology, i.e., Theorem 1.3. In Section 7, we extend the continuous dependence to the
immortal Ricci flow solutions, with which we prove Theorem 1.4 and Theorem 1.5. We also con-
struct examples where the bi-Hölder distance estimate cannot be improved to bi-Lipschitz distance
estimate. Then in Section 8, we prove the Kähler version of the pseudo-locality theorem, i.e., The-
orem 1.7, and apply it to prove Theorem 1.8. Finally, we discuss the future study and make several
conjectures in Section 9.

from USTC. The author would like to thank Weiyong He, Shaosai Huang, Haozhao Li, Yu Li and
Kai Zheng for helpful discussions. Particular thanks go to Shaosai Huang and Yu Li for carefully
reading the early versions of this manuscript and providing many helpful suggestions to improve
the exposition.

3 Rigidity and gap theorems

In this section, we first list some important properties of the Ricci flows(cf. Proposition 3.1,
Proposition 3.2, Corollary 3.3 and Proposition 3.4). They are implied or inspired by the proof of
pseudo-locality theorem of Perelman [83], with further improvements. Then in Theorem 3.5 and
Theorem 3.6, we use ν-entropy as a tool to generalize Anderson’s gap theorem and ǫ-regularity
theorem. We observe that Einstein condition is not essentially used and we are able to generalize
the gap theorem and the ǫ-regularity theorem for cscK metrics, which is achieved in Theorem 3.7
and Theorem 3.8. Finally, we obtain a compactness property for some moduli of cscK metrics in
Theorem 3.9.

    Let us reformulate Proposition 4.8 and Proposition 4.9 of [99] as the following rigidity proper-
ties.
Proposition 3.1 (Rigidity of Ricci flow in terms of µ). Suppose {(M m , g(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ T } is a Ricci
flow solution satisfying
                                               µ(M, g(0), T ) ≥ 0.                                     (3.1)
Then the flow is the static flow on Euclidean space (Rm , g           E ).


Proof. This is nothing but reformulation of Proposition 4.8 of [99].                                      

Proposition 3.2 (Rigidity of Riemannian manifolds in terms of ν). Suppose (M m , g) is a com-
plete Riemannian manifold with bounded curvature satisfying

                                                    ν(M, g, T ) ≥ 0                                    (3.2)

for some T > 0. Then (M, g) is isometric to the Euclidean space (Rm , gE ).

                                                          20
Proof. As (M, g) has bounded curvature, we can run Ricci flow from (M, g) for a short time period
δ0 for some δ0 ∈ (0, T ]. By Proposition 4.9 of [99], we have

                                     ν(M, g, δ0 ) ≤ µ(M, g, δ0 ) ≤ 0.                          (3.3)
On the other hand, since δ0 ∈ (0, T ], it follows from definition of ν and (3.2) that

                                     0 ≤ ν(M, g, T ) ≤ ν(M, g, δ0 ).                           (3.4)
Combining (3.4) with (3.3), we obtain
                                     ν(M, g, δ0 ) = µ(M, g, δ0 ) = 0.
As ν(M, g(t), δ0 − t) is monotonically nondecreasing along the flow, it is non-negative for each
t ∈ (0, δ0 ). Replacing g by g(t), δ0 by δ0 − t in the previous argument, we obtain
                      µ(M, g(t), δ0 − t) = ν(M, g(t), δ0 − t) = 0,            ∀t ∈ (0, δ0 ).
In particular, dtd µ(M, g(t), δ0 − t) = 0. Then we know that {(M, g(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ δ0 } satisfies the
shrinking gradient Ricci soliton equation
                                                              gi j (t)
                                        Ri j + f (t)i j −              ≡ 0.
                                                              δ0 − t
Then the bounded curvature condition of the flow implies that {(M, g(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ δ0 } must be
isometric to Euclidean space, as done in Proposition 4.8 and 4.9 of [99]. In particular, (M, g) is
isometric to Euclidean space.                                                                   

   By Lemma 2.5, the following corollary immediately follows from Proposition 3.2.
Corollary 3.3 (Rigidity of Riemannian manifolds in terms of ν and R). Suppose (M m , g) is a
complete Riemannian manifold with bounded curvature satisfying

                                        ν̄(M, g, T ) ≥ 0,          R ≥ 0,                      (3.5)
for some T > 0. Then (M, g) is isometric to the Euclidean space (Rm , gE ).

   A quantitative version of Proposition 3.1 is the following gap property for local µ-functional.
Proposition 3.4 (µ-gap of Ricci flow with bounded curvature). For each α ∈ (0, 1], H ∈ [1, ∞),
L ∈ [1, ∞), there is a constant δ = δ(α, m, H, L) with the following property.
   Suppose {(M m , x0 , g(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1} is a Ricci flow solution satisfying
                         
                          |Rm|(x, t) ≤ H, ∀ x ∈ Bg(t) (x0 , δ−1 ), t ∈ [0, 1];
                         
                         
                                                                                              (3.6)
                          µ(Bg(0) (x0 , δ−1 ), g(0), 1) ≥ −δ.
                         

Then we have
                                     sup           |Rm|(x, 1) < α,                             (3.7)
                                 x∈Bg(1) (x0 ,L)

                                      inf          |Bg(1) (x, 1)| ≥ (1 − α)ωm ,                (3.8)
                                 x∈Bg(1) (x0 ,L)

                                      inf          in j(x, 1) > α−1 .                          (3.9)
                                 x∈Bg(1) (x0 ,L)



                                                         21
Proof. Before we start the main stream of the proof, let us do a technical preparation. We observe
that under the condition (3.6), the lower bound of µ-functional implies a rough injectivity radius
estimate for points near x0 at positive time. In fact, by the local monotonicity formula (2.45) with
T = τT = 1 and proper choice of Ω′1 and Ω0 , we obtain
                                                        m
          ν(Bg(1) (x0 , 0.2δ−1 ), g(1), 1) ≥ −100δ2 − δ −  log 2 − 3m(m − 1)H ≥ −3m2 H.
                                                         2
Exploiting the curvature condition in (3.6), it follows from Theorem 2.8 that
                  r−m |B(x, r)|g(1) ≥ c(m, H),      ∀ r ∈ (0, 1], x ∈ Bg(1) (x0 , 0.1δ−1 ).
Therefore, by Cheeger-Gromov-Taylor [20], we obtain the desired rough injectivity radius esti-
mate
                        in j(x, 1) ≥ c(m, H), ∀ x ∈ Bg(1) (x0 , 0.1δ−1 ).              (3.10)

    Now we start the main stream of proof. We first show (3.7) via a contradiction argument.
If (3.7) were wrong, we could find a sequence of Ricci flow solutions {(Mi , xi , gi (t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1}
satisfying the condition (3.6) for number δi → 0 and violating the (3.7). In other words, for some
points zi ∈ Bgi (1) (xi , L), we have
                                           H ≥ |Rm|(zi , 1) ≥ α.                                   (3.11)
By the curvature estimate (3.11) and the rough injectivity radius estimate (3.10), we can apply
Hamilton’s compactness theorem(cf. [54]) to obtain
                                             C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
               {(Mi , xi , gi (t)), 0 < t ≤ 1} −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ {( M̄, x̄, ḡ(t)), 0 < t ≤ 1}.   (3.12)
In light of the local monotonicity formula (2.43), the lower bound of µ by −δi in (3.6) and the
above convergence together imply that µ( M̄, ḡ(0.5), 0.5) ≥ 0. Then it follows from Proposition 3.1
that the limit flow solution on interval [0.5, 1] is a static flow on Euclidean space (Rm , gE ). In
particular, we have
                                  lim |Rm|(zi , 1) = |Rm|(z̄, 1) = 0,
                                     i→∞
where z̄ is the limit of zi . The existence of z̄ is guaranteed by the fact that dgi (1) (zi , xi ) ≤ L
uniformly. The above equations mean that
                                               |Rm|(zi , 1) < α
for large i, which contradicts the assumption of the choice of zi in (3.11). Therefore, we finish the
proof of (3.7).
    The proof of (3.8) follows the same route as that of (3.7), up to an application of Bishop-
Gromov volume comparison, as almost-flat curvature bound is already provided by (3.7). Simi-
larly, one can prove (3.9). A key point is that when (3.7) holds(up to adjusting L to 2L if necessary),
in Bg(1) (x0 , L), we have conjugate radius bounded from below by 2α−1 under the condition (3.6).
If (3.9) fails at some point yi , we can find a smooth unit-speed geodesic lasso(cf. Lemma 5.6 of
Cheeger-Ebin [19]) γi : [0, ρi ] 7→ (Mi , gi (1)) with γi (0) = γi (ρi ) = yi and ρi ≤ 2α−1 . Combining
this inequality with (3.10), we obtain that c0 ≤ ρi ≤ 2α−1 . Passing to the limit along (3.12),
we obtain a unit-speed geodesic lasso on Euclidean space (Rm , gE ) with length ρ∞ ∈ [c0 , 2α−1 ],
which is absurd. This contradiction establishes the proof of (3.9). The proof of the proposition is
complete.                                                                                             

                                                      22
Theorem 3.5 (ν-gap of Einstein manifold). For each non-flat Einstein manifold (M m , g), we have

                                                        ν(M, g) ≤ −ǫ                                                    (3.13)

for some universal small constant ǫ = ǫ(m).

Proof. Let (M m , g) be a complete non-flat manifold such that ν(M, g) ≥ −ǫ. By the non-flatness,
we can find a point x0 ∈ M such that |Rm|(x0 ) , 0. Since ν(M, g) is scaling invariant, up to
rescaling, we can assume |Rm|(x0 ) = 1. Fix L >> 1. In the ball Ω = B(x0 , 2L), let y0 be a point
where |Rm|(·)d2 (·, ∂Ω) achieves the maximum value F 2 (See Figure 2). Let Q = |Rm|(y0 ). It is clear
that
                       F 2 = |Rm|(y0 )d2 (y0 , ∂Ω) ≥ |Rm|(x0 )d2 (x0 , ∂Ω) = 4L2 .
Let g̃ = Qg. Then we have |Rm|g̃ (y0 ) = 1 and dg̃ (y0 , ∂Ω) = F ≥ 2L. For each point x ∈ Bg̃ (y0 , L), it
follows from the triangle inequality that dg̃ (x, ∂Ω) ≥ F−L. Consequently, by the scaling invariance
of |Rm|d2 , we have

                                      |Rm|g̃ (y0 )dg̃2 (y0 , ∂Ω)              F2                  1
                       |Rm|g̃ (x) ≤                                     ≤             2
                                                                                          =            2 ≤ 4,         (3.14)
                                               dg̃2 (x, ∂Ω)                 (F − L)            1 − FL

for each point x ∈ Bg̃ (y0 , L). Note that

                                    ν(Bg̃ (y0 , L), g̃) ≥ ν(M, g̃) = ν(M, g) ≥ −ǫ.                                      (3.15)

Combining (3.14) and (3.15), we can apply Theorem 2.8 to obtain volume lower bound for
each unit geodesic ball contained in Bg̃ (y0 , L). Consequently, it follows from Cheeger-Gromov-
Taylor [20] that
                                      in jg̃ (x) > c0 (m),          ∀ x ∈ Bg̃ (y0 , L − 1).                             (3.16)

   Now we finish the proof of the theorem through a blowup argument. If the theorem were
wrong, we could find a sequence of non-flat Einstein manifolds (Mi , gi ) satisfying ν(Mi , gi ) ≥ −ǫi
with ǫi → 0. For each i, we choose Li such that Li → ∞. By the above discussion, modulo
rescaling if necessary, we can find yi ∈ Mi such that

                                                       |Rm|gi (yi ) = 1,                                                (3.17)

and
                 sup         |Rm|gi (x) ≤ 4,          inf          in jgi (x) ≥ c0 ,      ν(B(yi , Li ), gi ) ≥ −ǫi .   (3.18)
              x∈B(yi ,Li )                        x∈B(yi ,Li −1)

Since each gi is an Einstein manifold, we can obtain the harmonic radius estimate by Anderson [1].
In each harmonic coordinate, the Einstein equation can be applied to obtain uniform higher order
derivative estimates of the metrics. Consequently, we have the following compactness property:

                                                  C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                                  (Mi , yi , gi ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , y∞ , g∞ ),                                   (3.19)

where the limit is a smooth manifold. We claim that
                                            ν(M∞ , g∞ ) ≥ 0.                                    (3.20)

                               i→∞                     i→∞              i→∞

                                ν(M∞ , g∞ ) = inf µ(M∞ , g∞ , τ) ≥ 0,

which is (3.20). In particular, ν(M∞ , g∞ , 1) ≥ ν(M∞ , g∞ ) ≥ 0. Then it follows from Proposition 3.2
that (M∞ , g∞ ) is isometric to (Rm , gE ). In particular, we have |Rm|g∞ (y∞ ) = 0 which contradicts
(3.17) via the C ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov convergence.                                                     

   Theorem 3.5 can be regarded as a generalization of the famous gap theorem of Anderson for
Ricci-flat manifolds(cf. Lemma 3.1 of [1]), in terms of asymptotical volume ratio. A quantitative
version of Theorem 3.5 is the following ǫ-regularity theorem.
Theorem 3.6 (ǫ-regularity of Einstein manifold). Suppose (M m , x0 , g) is an Einstein manifold
satisfying                 
                            ν̄(B(x0 , 1), g, 1) ≥ −ǫ;
                           
                           
                                                                                        (3.21)
                            Rc(z) = c · g(z), ∀ z ∈ B(x0 , 1).
                           


                                                       24
Here ǫ is the small constant in Theorem 3.5, c is a constant satisfying |c| ≤ m − 1. Then for each
non-negative integer k, there exists a constant Ck = Ck (m) such that

                                       sup |∇k Rm|d2+k (z, ∂B(x0 , 1)) ≤ Ck .                                      (3.22)
                                    z∈B(x0 ,1)




Proof. It suffices to prove (3.22) for the case k = 0, the higher order(i.e. k ≥ 1) estimates follow
from the estimate when k = 0, via elliptic regularity of Einstein manifolds in harmonic coordinate
charts. Therefore, we focus on the proof of

                                          sup |Rm|(z)d2 (z, ∂B(x0 , 1)) ≤ C.                                       (3.23)
                                       z∈B(x0 ,1)

Let y0 be a point in B(x0 , 1) where the supreme
                                          p      of the left hand side of (3.23), which we denote by

F0 , is achieved. Let L0 ≔ 0.5F0 = 0.5 |Rm|(y0 )d(y0 , ∂B(x0 , 1)) and let Q0 ≔ |Rm|(y0 ).
   We now prove (3.23) by a contradiction argument. If (3.23) were wrong, we could find a
sequence (Mi , xi , gi ) satisfying (3.21) but violating (3.23). Then we have

                             Qi ≥ Fi2 = |Rm|(yi )d2 (yi , ∂B(xi, 1)) = 4L2i → ∞;                                   (3.24)

                                        −1

Let g̃i ≔ Qi gi . Notice that, in light of Proposition 2.1, the relationship (3.26) implies that
                                                                 −1
              ν̄(Bg̃i (yi , Li ), g̃i , L2i ) ≥ ν̄(Bgi (yi , Li Qi 2 ), gi , 1) ≥ ν̄(Bgi (xi , 1), gi , 1) ≥ −ǫ.

In summary, we have
                                         |Rm|g̃i (yi ) = 1;                                                        (3.27)

Around yi , the Ricci curvatures are tending to zero:

                                |Rc|g̃i (z) = Q−1
                                               i ci → 0,              ∀ z ∈ Bg̃i (0) (yi , Li ).                   (3.30)

Similar to the proof of Theorem 3.5, the harmonic radius in concern is uniformly bounded from
below. In harmonic coordinate, the Einstein condition implies that the metrics satisfy a strictly
elliptic PDE. For each positive integer k, the standard elliptic PDE estimate implies that the k-th
order curvature derivatives around yi are uniformly bounded. As discussed from (3.15) to (3.16),
we know that (3.29) provides a uniform lower bound of injectivity radius. So we have
                                                    C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                               (Mi, yi , g̃i ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , y∞ , g̃∞ ) ,

                                                                 25
where M∞ is a smooth manifold. It follows from the smooth convergence, (3.27) and (3.30) that

                                            |Rm|g̃∞ (y∞ ) = 1;                                    (3.31)
                                            Rcg̃∞ ≡ 0.                                            (3.32)

Following the same argument as in the proof of Theorem 3.5, the condition (3.29) implies that

                               ν̄ (M∞ , g̃∞ ) = inf ν̄ (M∞ , g̃∞ , τ) ≥ −ǫ.
                                              τ∈(0,∞)

In view of this entropy lower bound and the Ricci-flatness (3.32), we can apply Theorem 3.5 to
obtain that (M∞ , g̃∞ ) is actually isometric to the Euclidean space (Rm , gE ). In particular, we have
|Rm|g̃∞ (y∞ ) = 0, which contradicts (3.31). The proof of (3.23) and consequently the proof of the
theorem is complete.                                                                                  

   Checking the proof of Theorem 3.5 and Theorem 3.6, it is clear that the Einstein condition is
only used for regularity improvement and bounding scalar curvature. For critical metrics with
these two properties, one can easily follow the same argument to obtain gap theorem and ǫ-
regularity theorem. For example, we have the analogies of Theorem 3.5 and Theorem 3.6 for
cscK manifolds.

Theorem 3.7 (ν-gap of cscK manifold). For each non-flat cscK manifold (M n , g, J), we have

                                             ν(M, g) ≤ −ǫ                                         (3.33)

for some universal small constant ǫ = ǫ(n).

Proof. The strategy for the proof is the same as that in Theorem 3.5. The only difference is the
step to obtain the smooth convergence (3.19). We shall replace the harmonic coordinate estimate
by holomorphic coordinate estimate in the current setting.
   As discussed in the proof of Theorem 3.5, for each non-flat Riemannian manifold (M, g) satis-
fying ν(M, g) > −ǫ and each fixed constant L >> 1, up to rescaling, we can choose a base point
x0 ∈ M such that

                      |Rm|(x0 ) = 1,                                                              (3.34)
                  sup |Rm|(x) ≤ 4,           inf        in j(x) ≥ c0 ,   ν(B(x0 , L), g) ≥ −ǫ.    (3.35)
                x∈B(x0 ,L)               x∈B(x0 ,L−1)


Now we apply the condition that (M, g, J) is cscK. Note that in holomorphic coordinate chart, we
have the equation                
                                  Rαβ̄ = −∂α ∂β̄ log det gγλ̄ ,
                                 
                                 
                                                                                         (3.36)
                                  R = −∆g log det gγλ̄ .
                                 

Since ∇R ≡ 0, one can apply Schauder estimate to obtain a uniform size c with the following
properties: each ball B(y, c) ⊂ B(x0 , L − 1) admits a holomorphic chart {z1 , · · · , zn } such that the
                                         1
metric g in this chart have uniform C 2, 2 -norm(cf. Proposition 1.2 of Tian-Yau [96]). By the first
inequality in (3.35), the scalar curvature in concern is a constant which is uniformly bounded by

                                                    26
                                                                                                1
C(n). Therefore, applying the bootstrapping argument on (3.36) then implies uniform C k, 2 -norm
estimate of g for each k. In particular, we have

                                |∇k Rm|(z) ≤ Ck ,    ∀ z ∈ B(x0 , L − 1).                           (3.37)

   Then we are ready to finish the proof of this theorem. If the theorem were wrong, we could
find a sequence of non-flat cscK manifolds (Mi , gi , Ji ) satisfying ν(Mi , gi ) ≥ −ǫi with ǫi → 0. For
each i, we choose Li such that Li → ∞ and (3.34) and (3.35) hold, if we replace x0 by xi , replace
L by Li . The cscK condition implies (3.37) holds, if we replace x0 by xi . Therefore, it follows
from (3.34), (3.35) and (3.37) that the C ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov convergence (3.19) hold. Same as
the proof in Theorem 3.5, we know ν(M∞ , g∞ ) = 0 and consequently (M∞ , g∞ ) is isometric to
(R2n , gE ). Therefore, it follows from (3.34) and the previous discussion that
                                0 = |Rm|g∞ (x∞ ) = lim |Rm|gi (xi ) = 1.
                                                     i→∞

This contradiction establishes the proof of the theorem.                                                

fying                           
                                 ν̄(B(x0 , 1), g, 1) ≥ −ǫ;

                                 R(z) = c, ∀ z ∈ B(x0 , 1).
                                

Here ǫ is the small constant in Theorem 3.7, c is a constant satisfying |c| ≤ 2(n + 1). Then for each
non-negative integer k, there exists a constant Ck = Ck (n) such that

                                 sup |∇k Rm|d2+k (z, ∂B(x0 , 1)) ≤ Ck .                             (3.39)
                               z∈B(x0 ,1)




Proof. Following the strategy of the proof of Theorem 3.6, we can reduce the estimate (3.39) to
Theorem 3.7. The key point is the regularity improvement, which follows from the bootstrap-
ping of the equation (3.36) in holomorphic chart. Then the proof is almost the same as that in
Theorem 3.6. We leave the details for interested readers.                                     

   As an application of Theorem 3.8, we have the following compactness property.
Theorem 3.9 (Compactness of a subset of cscK moduli). There exists ǭ = ǭ(n) sufficiently small
such that the following properties hold.
   Let McscK (n, r0 , ǭ, σ, D) be the moduli space of cscK metrics (M n , g, J) satisfying

                                   inf I(B(x, r0 ), g) ≥ (1 − ǭ)I(R2n ),                           (3.40)
                                  x∈M
                                  sup |R| ≤ σ,                                                      (3.41)
                                   M
                                  diam(M n , g) ≤ D.                                                (3.42)

Here I means the isoperimetric constant. Then the moduli McscK (n, r0 , ǭ, σ, D) is compact with
respect to the C ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov topology.

                                                    27
Proof. Define                                                     (r           )
                                          ǫ                              ǫ

                                                                        2σ
where ǫ is the small constant in Theorem 3.7. Clearly, ρ20 ≤ r02 . It follows from Lemma 2.9 that
                                                           
                    ν B(x, ρ20 ), g, ρ20 ≥ ν B(x, r0 ), g, r02 ≥ −0.5ǫ − σρ20 ≥ −ǫ.

Then it follows from the compactness theorem of Hamilton that McscK (n, r0 , ǭ, σ, D) is compact

   By Theorem 3.9, if (Mi , gi , Ji ) ∈ McscK (n, r0 , ǭ, σ, D), then by taking subsequence if necessary,
we have
                                              C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                            (Mi , gi , Ji ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ).                    (3.43)
Let σi be the scalar curvature of (Mi , gi , Ji ), then σi → σ∞ . If we drop the constant scalar curvature
condition, can we still have a compactness theorem? We shall finally achieve this in Theorem 8.9,
in a weaker sense that the convergence topology is C 1,α -Cheeger-Gromov topology, rather than
the C ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov topology in (3.43). Furthermore, if the limit metric is smooth, then the
precise upper and lower bound of the scalar curvature is continuous under such convergence. Note
that in this case we do not have the regularity improvement property like Theorem 3.9, as the
underlying metric need not to satisfy a critical PDE. Instead, we apply the Ricci flow to construct
holomorphic coordinates and then develop a priori estimates. All such estimates will be built
on the pseudo-locality theorems with several more technical steps. In the proof of Theorem 3.6
and Theorem 3.8, the rigidity property Proposition 3.2 plays an important role. Correspondingly,
the rigidity property Proposition 3.1 will play a similar role in the proof of the pseudo-locality
theorems. Therefore, we can regard Proposition 3.1 and its quantitative version, Proposition 3.4,
as the prelude of the pseudo-locality theorems in Ricci flow. Then Theorem 3.6 and Theorem 3.8
are nothing but the baby models of the pseudo-locality theorems in the Riemannian and the Kähler
Ricci flow.


4 The pseudo-locality theorems in the Ricci flow

In this section, we shall prove the improved pseudo-locality, Theorem 1.2. The basic idea of the
proof follows from Perelman [83]. However, as the conditions and consequences in Theorem 1.2
refine those of Perelman, we shall adjust the organizations of Perelman’s proof to include more
intermediate steps, which are important for the further generalization of the pseudo-locality theo-
rem under the Kähler setting in Section 8. Based on the gap constant chosen in Proposition 3.4,
we actually do not need further blowup argument in the proof of Theorem 1.2. Consequently, if
the constant in Proposition 3.4 is explicitly calculated, then all the constants in Theorem 1.2 can

                                                        28
also be calculated. Therefore, our proof provides an approach for estimating the gap constant in
Theorem 1.2 explicitly.
    We briefly describe the structure of this section. In Proposition 4.1, we apply traditional max-
imum principle to choose a well-controlled space-time domain in the Ricci flow where Proposi-
tion 3.4 can be applied. Based on the existence of such a domain, we then show in Proposition 4.3
that a rough bound of curvature and a delicate bound of entropy together imply curvature improve-
ment. Consequently, this improvement can be combined with a maximum principle type argument
to show that the pseudo-locality theorem holds with rough coefficients, which is done in Theo-
rem 4.4. Then we apply Proposition 3.4 again, with better coefficients, to improve Theorem 4.4
to Theorem 4.5, Corollary 4.6 and Corollary 4.7, which are refined versions of the pseudo-locality
theorem. Then we discuss how to relate our version of pseudo-locality theorem with the exist-
ing versions. In Lemma 4.10, we show that the condition (1.4) is natural and can be obtained
from Cheeger-Colding theory if Ricci curvature is bounded from below. Then we relate versions
of Perelman and Tian-Wang to our current versions of pseudo-locality theorem in Corollary 4.11
and 4.12. Combining all the discussions, we finally conclude the proof of Theorem 1.2 at the end
of this section.

Remark 4.2. The method we applied to find the well-behaved space-time domain H in Proposi-
tion 4.1 can be naturally regarded as a parabolic version of the one in Theorem 3.5, as illustrated
by Figure 2 and Figure 3. In Perelman [83], such choice was achieved through an iterating
point-selecting method. Here we follow a more traditional route to achieve a similar purpose,
by choosing a proper auxiliary function and selecting maximum value point. Furthermore, the
domain H obtained here is always in the “central” part, which property is not available in Perel-
man’s original proof and is important for our improvement of Perelman’s pseudo-locality theorem.


   The reason we choose constant αm2 A in condition (4.7) is for the purpose of maximum principle
and contradiction argument. Roughly speaking, under the condition F 2 ≤ αm A in a small domain,
we can apply the entropy gap property to improve the estimate to F 2 ≤ αm2 A in a large domain.
Following the notation in Proposition 3.4 , by setting α = 0.5 and H = L = 4, we define

                                       Am ≔ max α−5 m  , α −4 −2
                                                             δ
                                                           m m .                                (4.26)

   Note that Theorem 4.4 is an intermediate step for Theorem 4.5, the pseudo-locality theorem
with refined coefficients. This step is technically necessary for the application of Proposition 4.3,
where one can improve curvature estimate in a “big” domain whenever there is a “large” curvature
point appears in the “central” space-time. If we try to prove (4.44) firstly by contradiction, then
Proposition 4.3 cannot be applied to obtain a priori curvature bound in a “big” domain, which
prevents the further application of Proposition 3.4.

                ≥ ν Bg(0) x0 , 20A t , g(0), T ≥ ν Bg(0) x0 , 20A T , g(0), T .

                              √                          √          
            inf µ Bg(0) x0 , 20A t , g(0), t ≥ ν Bg(0) x0 , 20A T , g(0), T ≥ −A−2 .

Remark 4.8. One can regard Corollary 4.6 as the “almost local” version, or the quantitative
version, of the rigidity property Proposition 3.2, in terms of the ν-functional. Theorem 4.5 is the
further localization of Corollary 4.6 and it is the “almost local” version of Proposition 3.1 in
terms of µ-functional. In short, the pseudo-locality theorems can be illustrated as quantitative
versions of rigidity properties.
Remark 4.9. In Theorem 4.5, Corollary 4.6 and Corollary 4.7, for each small α, we can choose
A large enough such that (4.44) holds for the given α. For simplicity of notation, we shall replace
α by ψ(A−1 |m) in future discussion.

   The condition (4.43) looks clumsy. However, we shall see from the following lemma that it is
natural under the condition of Ricci curvature being uniformly bounded from below.
Lemma 4.10 (µ by almost Euclidean volume ratio and Ricci curvature lower bound). For
each pair of positive numbers (η, A), there exists a constant ξ = ξ(η, m, A) with the following
property.
   Suppose
                                                                  
                            Rc(x, 0) ≥ −(m − 1)ξ, ∀ x ∈ B x0 , ξ −1 ;                         (4.57)
                                          
                            ξ m B x0 , ξ −1 ≥ (1 − ξ)ωm .                                     (4.58)
Then we have
                                    µ̄(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) ≥ −0.5η,                            (4.59)
                                    µ(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) ≥ −η.                                (4.60)


                                                  38
Proof. We shall show (4.59) first and then proceed to show (4.60).
   The proof of (4.59) basically follows from the proof of Proposition 3.1 of Tian-Wang [95]. We
repeat the key steps here, using the local functionals, for the convenience of the readers.
     For otherwise, we can choose a violating sequence for ξi → 0 satisfying (4.57) and (4.58) for
ξi . However, (4.59) is violated. By Cheeger-Colding theory, we can assume that
                                     pointed−Gromov−Hausdor f f
                        (Mi , xi , di ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , x∞ , d∞ ).
By volume convergence and Bishop-Gromov volume comparison, it is not hard to see that the
asymptotic volume ratio of M∞ is ωm . This force that M∞ is actually isometric to (Rm , gE ). Sup-
pose ϕi is a minimizer function for µ̄(B(xi, 20A), gi , 1). Then ϕi satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equa-
tion                                                        √ 
                         −2∆ϕi − 2ϕi log ϕi − m 1 + log 4π ϕi = µ̄i ϕi ,                        (4.61)
for µ̄i = µ̄(B(xi, 20A), gi , 1) ≤ −η. Note that the Sobolev constant estimate and the Moser iteration
argument guarantee that C1 ≤ ϕi,max ≤ C and µ̄i ≥ −C uniformly. Furthermore, there are uniform
interior Lipschitz estimate and uniform boundary Hölder estimate, as applications of Cheng-Yau’s
estimate on the above equation. Then we can show that ϕi converges to a limit function ϕ∞ which
is Lipschitz in each compact subset of B(x∞, 20A) and vanishes on ∂B(x∞, 20A). Furthermore, by
taking limit of (4.61), we can show that ϕ∞ satisfies the equation
                                                             √ 
                        −2∆ϕ∞ − 2ϕ∞ log ϕ∞ − m 1 + log 4π ϕ∞ = µ̄∞ ϕ∞ ,

for some −∞ < µ̄∞ ≤ −η in the distribution sense on B(x∞ , 20A). However, the above equation
contradicts the Logarithmic Sobolev inequality in (Rm , gE ) since

                               ϕ∞ ∈ W01,2 (B(x∞ , 20A)) ⊂ W01,2 (Rm ).

Therefore, we finish the proof of (4.59).
   We continue to show (4.60). Actually, it follows from (2.17) in Lemma 2.5 that

                   µ(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) − µ̄(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) ≥ R ≥ −m(m − 1)ξ.              (4.62)

The R above means the lower bound of the scalar curvature. Note that ξ is very small such that
B(x0 , 20A) ⊂ B(x0 , ξ −1 ), which fact together with (4.57) are used in the deduction of the above
inequality. By choosing ξ even smaller, we can also assume that 2m(m − 1)ξ ≤ 0.5η. Plugging this
inequality into (4.62), we obtain

                          µ(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) ≥ µ̄(B(x0 , 20A), g, 1) − 0.5η.

Therefore, (4.60) follows from the combination of the above inequality with (4.59). The proof the
lemma is complete.                                                                              

   Note that in Lemma 4.10, we prove (4.59) first and then prove (4.60). This order is important.
Technically, one need uniform Lipschitz or Hölder continuity of minimizer functions to obtain a
limit function. To achieve such bounds, µ̄ is more convenient than µ as it does not contain the scalar
curvature term R. Actually, in order to develop Lipschitz or Hölder continuity for minimizers of
µ, one needs extra information of ∇R, which is hard to obtain in general.

   By Corollary 4.11 and Corollary 4.12, we see that the pseudo-locality of Perelman and Tian-
Wang can be unified in terms of the local entropy, without using the deep analysis from RCD
theory and mass transportation by Cavalletti-Mondino [15].

                                                      41
5 Distance distortion and topological stability

In this section, we localize the distance distortion estimate in section 4 of Tian-Wang [95] and
provide topological applications. The basic idea is that when the volume element is decreasing
and the distance is expanding, we shall have a rough distance distortion estimate along the flow,
if the initial volume ratio has an upper bound. This estimate can be refined if we further have the
                                  R 1R
almost Einstein condition, i.e., 0 Ω |R|dvdt is very small for some fixed domain Ω. Under the
condition of Tian-Wang’s version of the pseudo-locality theorem(cf. Corollary 4.12), if the initial
time slice has almost Euclidean volume ratio, then the later time slice is almost Euclidean too. Fix
a unit geodesic ball Ω at the initial time. On the one hand, the loss of its volume along the flow is
                R1R
measured by 0 Ω |R|dvdt. On the other hand, the “size” of the set Ω is expanding along the flow,
which together with the almost Euclidean condition at both time-slices make the volume almost
                                                         R 1R
increasing. The combination of these two effects force 0 Ω |R|dvdt to be very small. Namely, the
almost Einstein condition holds locally on Ω. Therefore, the distance distortion estimate follows
directly from section 4 of [95]. Although no new idea beyond [95] is needed, here we shall
provide more details under more general conditions, which will be important for the topological
application(cf. Corollary 5.6) and further improvement in the Kähler setting(cf. Theorem 8.1).
    We briefly overview the structure of this section. We first develop a local maximum principle
in Theorem 5.1, which guarantees the almost non-negativity of scalar curvature in the setting of
the pseudo-locality theorem. This step is necessary for showing the almost-decreasing property of
volume element. Then we follow the idea described above to obtain the locally almost Einstein
condition and the distance distortion estimate(cf. Proposition 5.3 and Theorem 5.4). Combining
these with Hochard’s method, we obtain Ricci flow solutions with life span estimate and distance
distortion estimate(cf. Corollary 5.5). In particular, starting from a complete Riemannian mani-
fold with non-negative Ricci curvature and almost Euclidean asymptotic volume ratio, we obtain
an immortal Ricci flow solution(cf. Corollary 5.6), which is used to construct a diffeomorphism
from M to Rm in Theorem 5.7. Such a flow can also be used to show global rigidities of (M, g)
(cf. Theorem 5.8) and local estimates of manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded below(cf. Theo-
rem 5.9). Finally, we analyze the asymptotic behavior of the immortal solution and show it must
diverge at infinity(cf. Proposition 5.12, Proposition 5.14 and Corollary 5.15), justifying that the
distance distortion estimate is optimal, at least on non-compact manifolds. Near the discussions
on rigidities, we also define some regularity radii(cf. Definition 5.10) for later applications.

   The following local maximum principle is motivated by Theorem 3.1 of B.L.Chen [22]. It
will be used in this section to show the almost non-negativity of the scalar curvature and later in
Section 8 to show the almost non-increasing of the maximum value of the scalar curvature.

   Theorem 5.1 is a localized version of the maximum principle for sub-heat-solution. On a
Ricci flow solution with Ricci curvature bounded, a bounded sub-heat-solution f will satisfy the
maximum principle:
                                 f (x, t) ≤ sup f (·, 0), ∀ x ∈ M.
                                                        M
This can also be obtained from Theorem 5.1 by adding f by a constant and letting A → ∞.

If (M, g) has bounded curvature, we may start the Ricci flow for a short time period. In light
of (5.38), we can apply Theorem 1.2 through the condition (1.8). This pseudo-locality theorem
implies that this flow existence time must be at least T . The bounded curvature condition can
be dropped by exploiting the method of Hochard [57]. See also [56] and [67] for more details.
Actually, we can find a sequence of cutoff functions 0 ≤ ηk = e−2 fk ≤ 1 whose support set is
Uk = B(x0 , 2k) for some fixed x0 ∈ M and ηk ≡ 1 on Dk = B(x0 , k). Moreover, we can ensure that
η−1
 k satisfies some uniform bounds(see (2.5) of [67]). Clearly, we have

                                        Dk ⋐ U k ,   M = ∪∞
                                                          k=1 Dk .                                 (5.39)
