           k g = e g. Then (U k , hk ) becomes a complete Riemannian manifold with |Rm|
bounded by Ck , a constant depending on (Uk , g). The scalar curvature of (Uk , hk ) is uniformly
bounded from below
                                         Rhk ≥ −Λ,                                          (5.40)

                                                     49
which is independent of k(see (2.2) of [56] or (2.27) of [67]). Furthermore, fixing ξ very small,
there exists an r0 independent of k such that

                                  e2 fk (y)−ξ g ≤ hk ≤ e2 fk (y)+ξ g,   on Bhk (y, r0 ).                           (5.41)
Namely, the metric hk and e2 fk (y) g are quasi-isometric to each other in the ball Bhk (y, r0 ). In [56]
and [67], we are dealing with almost Euclidean isoperimetric condition for Perelman’s pseudo-
locality. Suppose each r0 -ball under metric g has almost Euclidean isoperimetric constant, then
the quasi-isometric condition (5.41) is sufficient to guarantee that each r0 -ball under hk has almost
Euclidean isoperimetric constant(see (2.31) of [67]). However, here we only have a weaker con-
dition (5.34). Then Theorem 1.2, the improved pseudo-locality theorem shall take charge through
the µ-functional condition (1.4). Actually, it follows from (5.41) that

                Bhk (y, r) ⊂ Be2 fk (y) g (y, 2r) = Bg (y, 2e− fk (y) r) ⊂ Bg (y, 2r), ∀ r ∈ (0, r0 ]. (5.42)

   A particularly interesting case of Corollary 5.5 is T = ∞ and Rc ≥ 0.

Corollary 5.6 (ν̄-entropy and the immortal Ricci flow). Suppose (M, g) is a complete Rieman-
nian manifold satisfying
                               Rc ≥ 0, ν̄(M, g) ≥ −ǫ ≥ −ǭ(m),
then there exists an immortal Ricci flow solution {(M, g(t)), 0 ≤ t < ∞} initiated from g satisfying
(5.35), (5.36) and (5.37) with T = ∞.

    The immortal Ricci flow solution in Corollary 5.6 provides a new perspective to understand
the geometry and topology of the initial Riemannian manifold (M, g). For example, we have the
following topological consequence, which is known by Cheeger-Colding [18].

   The immortal Ricci flow solution in Corollary 5.6 can also help us to show geometry rigidity
properties related to (M, g).
Theorem 5.8 (Equivalence of several rigidities). Suppose (M, g) is a complete Riemannian man-
ifold with Rc ≥ 0. Then we have the equivalence relationship

                      ν̄(M, g) = 0 ⇔ I(M, g) = I(Rm , gE ) ⇔ AVR(M, g) = ωm .                         (5.51)

Furthermore, one of the identities hold in (5.51) if and only if (M, g) is isometric to (Rm , gE ).

                                                       53
Proof. It suffices to show each identity is equivalent to the manifold (M m , g) being isometric
to the Euclidean space. The asymptotic volume ratio version and isoperimetric constant ver-
sion are well-known. Therefore, we only need to show that ν̄(M, g) = 0 implies (M, g) is iso-
metric to the Euclidean metric. By Corollary 5.6, there exists an immortal solution initiated
from (M, g) with curvature estimate (5.35). Applying the local monotonicity formula for ν-
functional(cf.Theorem 2.10), for each t > 0, we have

                           ν(M, g(t)) ≥ ν(M, g(0)) = ν(M, g) ≥ ν̄(M, g) = 0,

where we used the fact Rg ≥ 0 in the last inequality. In light of Proposition 3.2, (M, g(t)) is
isometric to the Euclidean metric. Let t → 0, the continuity of metrics g(t) then yields that (M, g)
is isometric to (Rm , gE ).                                                                       

   The quantitive version of Theorem 5.8 is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.9 (Equivalence of several quantitive rigidities). Suppose (M m , g) is a complete Rie-
mannian manifold and B(x0 , 1) ⊂ M is a geodesic ball with Rc ≥ −2(m − 1).

  (a) For each small ǫ, there exists δ = δ(ǫ, m) such that if log |B(xrm0 ,r)| ≥ −δ for some r ∈ (0, δ),
      then I(B(x0 , δr)) ≥ (1 − ǫ)I(Rm ).

  (b) For each small δ, there exists ξ = ξ(δ, m) such that if I(B(x0 , r)) ≥ (1 − ξ)I(Rm ) for some
      r ∈ (0, ξ), then ν̄(B(x0 , r)) ≥ −δ.

  (c) For each small ξ, there exists ǫ = ǫ(ξ, m) such that if ν̄(B(x0 , r), r2 ) ≥ −ǫ for some r ∈ (0, ǫ),
      then log |B(xρm0 ,ρ)| ≥ −ξ for each ρ ∈ (0, ξr).


Proof. It is clear that (a) follows from the work of Cavalletti-Mondino [15], and (b) follows from
Lemma 2.9. We now focus on the proof of (c). Suppose ν̄(B(x0 , r), r2 ) ≥ −ǫ for some r ∈ (0, ǫ).
Let g̃ = (ξr)−2 g. Then in the ball Bg̃ (x0 , ξ −1 ) ⊂ M, we have

                                Rcg̃ ≥ −2(m − 1)ξ 2 r2 ≥ −2(m − 1)ξ 2 ǫ 2 ,                        (5.52)
                                           −1        −2
                                ν̄(Bg̃ (x0 , ξ ), g̃, ξ ) ≥ −ǫ.                                    (5.53)

nian manifold ( M̂, ĝ) with bounded curvature. The point is to choose a cutoff function η which is
identically 1 inside Bg̃ (x0 , 2) and vanishes outside Bg̃ (x0 , 4). Then we set M̂ = {x ∈ M|η(x) < ∞}
          1
and ĝ = e η g̃. Under this construction, it is clear that

                                          ĝ = g̃,   on Bg̃ (x0 , 2).                              (5.54)

Explicit calculation shows that ( M̂, ĝ) is complete, has bounded curvature and satisfies

                                             Rĝ ≥ −ψ(ǫ|m, ξ).                                     (5.55)

( M̂, ĝ) is ψ(ǫ|ξ, m)-close to a small ball in Bg̃ (x0 , 4) up to rescaling(cf. (5.41)). Using the same
argument as that in the proof of Corollary 5.5, we can apply Proposition 2.7 to obtain that
                                                      

                            r∈(0,ξ−1 ]

period. In light of (5.55) and (5.56), we can apply the pseudo-locality theorem to guarantee
that

   Because of Theorem 5.9, we are ready to define some radii for the convenience of further study.
Definition 5.10. Suppose (M, g) is a closed manifold and ǫ0 = ǫ0 (m) is sufficiently small. Then we
define                                 
                        vrǫ0 (x) ≔ inf r > 0|r−m |B(x, r)| < (1 − ǫ0 )ωm ,
                                       n                           o
                        erǫ0 (x) ≔ inf r > 0|ν̄(B(x, r), r2 ) < −ǫ0 ,
                                       
                        Irǫ0 (x) ≔ inf r > 0|I(B(x, r)) < (1 − ǫ0 )I(Rm ) .
They are called volume radius, entropy radius and isoperimetric radius respectively. Accordingly,
we define
                                   vrǫ0 (M, g) ≔ inf vrǫ0 (x, g),
                                                             x∈M
                                            erǫ0 (M, g) ≔ inf erǫ0 (x, g),
                                                             x∈M
                                            Irǫ0 (M, g) ≔ inf Irǫ0 (x, g).
                                                             x∈M
When no confusion is caused, we may omit ǫ0 and g.

                                                            55
Remark 5.11. Suppose (M, g) is an Einstein manifold with |Rc| ≤ m − 1, by the regularity im-
provement of metrics under elliptic PDE, we can apply Theorem 5.9 to obtain

                           vrǫ ≤ C(m)Irδ ≤ C(m)erξ ≤ C(m)vrǫ ′ ≤ C(m)vrǫ .

Therefore, the different radii defined above are all equivalent(cf. Proposition 2.49 of [33] for more
information). Similar conclusions also hold on Ricci shrinkers(cf. Theorem 4.10 of [75]). How-
ever, for general Riemannian manifolds, vrǫ is hard to apply, due to the lack of Bishop-Gromov
volume comparison and a gap lemma of Anderson type(cf. [1]). It becomes natural to exploit the
entropy radius erξ instead to study the compactness of other critical metrics with bounded scalar
curvature, e.g. the cscK metrics and extK(extremal Kähler) metrics.

   Theorem 5.9 shows that quantitive rigidities hold for initial manifold. A natural question is
whether a quantitive rigidity holds for the flow initiated from (M, g). For example, shall the im-
mortal flow in Corollary 5.6 converge back to a Euclidean metric? Unfortunately, we shall show
that the flow must fail to converge.

which contradicts (5.62) for ρ = 1. This contradiction establishes the proof of the proposition.   

   In view of Corollary 5.15, the immortal Ricci flow solution initiated from (M, g) diverges at

                          1+ψ

which contradicts the failure of (5.71). Therefore, the distance distortion estimate (5.36) can
hardly be improved. In fact, we shall construct examples on closed Kähler manifolds in Section 7
to show that (5.36) can be achieved. Therefore, (5.36) can be regarded as optimal.


6 Continuous dependence on the initial data

The purpose of this section is to show Theorem 1.3, which says that the Ricci flow solution de-
pends on the initial data continuously in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology. We shall first show this
for unnormalized Ricci flow in Theorem 6.1, then we show the uniform volume continuity(cf.
Lemma 6.5) along the Ricci flow and prove a version for normalized Ricci flow in Theorem 6.6.

                                                          59
Theorem 6.1 (Continuous dependence in the C ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov topology). Suppose that
N = {(N m , h(t)), 0 ≤ t ≤ T } is a Ricci flow solution initiated from h = h(0), a smooth Riemannian
metric on a closed manifold N m . For each ǫ small, there exists δ = δ(N, ǫ) with the following
properties.
   Suppose (M m , g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying

                           Rc > −(m − 1)ǫ −1 ,           dGH {(M, g), (N, h)} < δ.             (6.1)

Then the Ricci flow initiated from (M, g) exists on [0, T ]. Furthermore, there exists a family of
diffeomorphisms {Φt : N → M, ǫ ≤ t ≤ T } such that

                                 sup Φ∗t g(t) − h(t) C[ǫ−1 ] (N,h(t)) < ǫ.                     (6.2)
                                t∈[ǫ,T ]




   In view of Theorem 6.1, if we take a sequence (Mim , gi ) converging to (N m , h) in the Gromov-
Hausdorff topology while keeping Ricci curvature bounded from below, then the existence time of
the Ricci flow initiated from (Mi , gi ) will be at least T and
                                                 C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                              (Mi, gi (t)) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M, g(t))                        (6.3)

for each t ∈ (0, T ], by taking subsequence if necessary.
    The proof of Theorem 6.1 needs three preliminary steps. We first construct diffeomorphism be-
tween locally almost flat manifolds with rough Gromov-Hausdorff approximations. This step has
nothing to do with flow. In step two, we show that the almost flatness and rough Gromov-Hausdorff
approximation conditions in Lemma 6.2 are naturally realized by the curvature-injectivity-radius
estimate in the improved pseudo-locality. Therefore, we are able to construct a diffeomorphism
map from N to M. Via this diffeomorphism map and time shifting, we can regard g(ξ) as a C 0 -
approximation of h for very small ξ. This second step will be done in Lemma 6.3. In the third
step, we use a fact(cf. Lemma 6.4) that the C 0 -approximation is preserved for a long period of
time along the Ricci-DeTurck flow with given background metric h. With these three steps, one
can finish the proof of Theorem 6.1 by the standard method of coupling Ricci-flow with harmonic
map flow.
Lemma 6.2 (Construction of diffeomorphisms). For each small ǫ > 0, there exists a constant δ
with the following properties.
   Suppose (M m , g) and (N m , h) are two complete Riemannian manifolds satisfying

                                           |Rm| ≤ δ2 ,        in j ≥ δ−1 .                     (6.4)

Suppose there exist (not necessarily continuous) maps F : M → N and G : N → M such that

                |d(x, y) − d(F(x), F(y))| < δ,         ∀ x, y ∈ M satisfying d(x, y) < 100;    (6.5)
                |d(x, y) − d(G(x), G(y))| < δ,         ∀ x, y ∈ N satisfying d(x, y) < 100;    (6.6)
                sup d(x, G ◦ F(x)) + sup d(y, F ◦ G(y)) < δ.                                   (6.7)
                x∈M                        y∈N

Then we have diffeomorphisms F̃ : M → N and G̃ : N → M such that

                                 sup F̃ ∗ h − g g (x) + sup G̃∗ g − h h (y) < ǫ.                            (6.8)
                                 x∈M                            y∈N



Proof. The proof originates from the celebrated work of Cheeger [17]. The diffeomorphisms F̃
and G̃ satisfying (6.8) can be constructed explicitly. We shall basically follow the construction
in Peters [84] and Green-Wu [47], where a uniform diameter of M and N is assumed. Checking
the proofs there carefully, it is not hard to see that the diameter bound is not essentially used for
deriving diffeomorphisms satisfying C 0 -norm estimate (6.8).
  Let {x1 , x2 , x3 · · · , xk , · · · } be points in M such that {B(xi, 1)}∞                         ∞

                           |d(yi , y j ) − d(xi , x j )| < δ,        whenever d(xi , x j ) < 10.

is a covering of N. For each i, let A(i) be the collection of j such that B(x j , 2) ∩ B(xi, 2) , ∅. By
volume comparison, it is easy to see that ♯{A(i)} < 5m .
   Following Peters [84], we let ui : Rm → T xi M and ūi : Rm → T yi N be linear isometries. Then
we set

                            φ̄i ≔ expyi ◦ ūi : B(0, 10) ⊂ Rm → B(yi , 10) ⊂ N.
For each j ∈ A(i), we denote the the parallel translation along the shortest geodesic connecting xi
and x j by Pi j . Similarly, the parallel translation along the shortest geodesic connecting yi and y j
is denoted by P̄i j . In light of the distance distortion estimate (6.5) and the curvature-injectivity-

                                          sup G̃∗ g − h h (x) < ψ.                                   (6.11)

Combining (6.10) and (6.11), by choosing δ sufficiently small, we arrive at (6.8).
    It remains to show that both F̃ and G̃ are diffeomorphisms. By symmetry, it suffices to show
that F̃ : M → N is a diffeomorphism. This can be realized by a standard argument(cf. [84], [5]
and the references therein). We write down a few more words for the convenience of the readers.
The center of mass construction guarantees that F̃, G̃ are just perturbations of F and G. Then it
follows from (6.7) that
                                                                  
                        sup d x, G̃ ◦ F̃(x) < ψ, sup d y, F̃ ◦ G̃(y) < ψ
                          x∈M                             y∈N

for some ψ = ψ(δ|m). In other words, both F̃ ◦ G̃ and G̃ ◦ F̃ are close to the identity map in the
C 0 -sense. By condition (6.5) and (6.6), G̃ ◦ F̃(x) locates in a small neighborhood of x where the
geometry is almost Euclidean and strong convexity holds. Therefore, for each x ∈ M, there exists
a unique shortest geodesic connecting x to G̃ ◦ F̃(x) for each x ∈ M. Therefore, G̃ ◦ F̃(x) is in
the trivial homotopy class and can be smoothly deformed to IdM→M . In particular, the degree of
G̃ ◦ F̃ is 1. Note that G̃ ◦ F̃ is non-degenerate by (6.10) and (6.11). This forces that for every point
y ∈ N, the preimage F̃ −1 (y) contains only one point. Since F̃ : M → N is a smooth covering map
by (6.9), it is clear that F̃ is a diffeomorphism from M to N. By reversing the role of F̃ and G̃, we
obtain that G̃ is a diffeomorphism from N to M.                                                       

    The stability of Ricci-DeTurck flow under C 0 -perturbation was first studied by M. Simon(cf.
Theorem 1.1. of [90]). For a given Ricci-DeTurck flow, the linearized equation of Ricci-DeTurck
flow is strictly parabolic. Applying the heat kernel of the given Ricci-Deturk space-time on lin-
earized equation, one can estimate more precisely how does the evolving metrics depend on the
initial metric. This was observed and achieved by Koch-Lamm (cf. Theorem 4.3 of [70]) when
the background metric is Euclidean. By further localization technique, this idea can be realized
on Ricci-DeTurck flow on manifolds with bounded curvature(cf. Corollary 3.4 of Burkhardt-
Guim [11]). The following lemma is a special case of the results of [90] or [11].

                           t∈[0.5ǫ,T ]                                     t+t0 g(t+t0 )



by adjusting L if necessary. Combining (6.22) and (6.24), by adjusting L again, we obtain

                                           sup Φ∗t g(t) − h(t) h(t) < Lξ.                         (6.25)
                                          t∈[ǫ,T ]

For each integer k ∈ [1, ǫ −1 + 1], we show that

                                         sup Φ∗t g(t) − h(t) Ck (N,h(t)) < ǫ                      (6.26)
                                     t∈[ǫ,T ]

whenever dGH ((M, g), (N, h)) < δ sufficiently small. For otherwise, we can find δi → 0 and
ti ∈ [ǫ, T ] such that
                                 Φ∗ti g(ti ) − h(ti ) Ck (N,h(t )) ≥ ǫ.               (6.27)
                                                                       i

By (6.23) and (6.25), we can take smooth limits of Φ∗ti g(ti ) and h(ti ) in each fixed normal coordinate
chart of (N, h(T )). Let g∞ (t∞ ) and h∞ (t∞ ) be the limits respectively. Since δi → 0, we can take
ξi → 0, then it follows from (6.25) that

                                                g∞ (t∞ ) − h∞ (t∞ ) ≡ 0,

which contradicts the smooth limit of (6.27). Therefore, the contradiction argument establishes
the proof of (6.26). Since k is an arbitrary positive constant less that ǫ −1 + 1, it is clear that (6.2)
follows from the combination of (6.26) and (6.25). The proof of the theorem is complete.               

   Then we proceed to generalize Theorem 6.1 to normalized Ricci flow. The following lemma is
the key for our generalization.

Lemma 6.5 (Uniform volume continuity). Same conditions as in Theorem 6.1. Suppose ǫ is
small enough such that |Rm|h ≤ ǫ −1 .

   For each small positive number ξ < 100 , there is a δ = δ(ξ, ǫ, h) with the following property:

7 Stability of immortal Ricci flow solution

In this section, we shall study the global behavior of the normalized Ricci flow near a given
immortal solution initiated from (N, h). For simplicity of notation, up to rescaling, we always
assume that (N, h) satisfies
                                           Rch > −2(m − 1).                                           (7.1)
The reason we use −2(m − 1) rather than −(m − 1) is to reserve rooms to perturb metrics nearby
space form metrics of sectional curvature −1, whose Ricci curvature is −(m − 1).

Definition 7.1. A closed Einstein metric (N, hE ) is called weakly stable if there is an ǫ = ǫ(N, hE )
with the following properties.
   For every smooth Riemannian metric h satisfying

                              |N|dvh = |N|dvhE ,    kh − hE kC[ǫ−1 ] (hE ) < ǫ,                       (7.2)

the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (N, h) exists immortally and converges(in smooth topol-
ogy) to an Einstein metric (N, h′E ).
   (N, hE ) is called strictly stable if each h′E is isometric to hE for some sufficiently small ǫ.

Theorem 7.2 (Stability nearby a stable Ricci flow). Suppose (N, h) is the initial metric of an
immortal solution of normalized Ricci flow which converges to a weakly stable Einstein manifold
(N, hE ). Then there exists a small constant ǫ = ǫ(N, h) with the following properties.
   If (M, g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying

                            Rcg > −2(m − 1),        dGH ((M, g), (N, h)) < ǫ,                         (7.3)

then the normalized Ricci flow solution initiated from (M, g) exists immortally and converges to an
Einstein manifold (N, h′E ). Furthermore, h′E is homothetic to hE if hE is strictly stable.

Proof. Since the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (N, h) exists immortally and converges to
(N, hE ), for each small ǫ, we may choose T = T (ǫ) such that

                                      kh(T ) − hE kC[ǫ−1 ] (h(T )) < ǫ.                               (7.4)

                                                    69
By the continuous dependence of the normalized Ricci flow on the initial data(cf. Theorem 6.6),
we can choose δ = δ(ǫ) such that if (M, g) satisfies

                            dGH ((M, g), (N, h)) < δ,      Rcg > −2(m − 1),

then the normalized Ricci flow initiated from g(0) = g exists on time interval [0, T ] and

                                    h(T ) − Φ∗ g(T ) C[ǫ−1 ] (h(T )) < ǫ                         (7.5)

for some diffeomorphism Φ : N → M. Combining (7.4) and (7.5), we know that Φ∗ g(T ) locates
in a 2ǫ-neighborhood of hE . Since hE is weakly stable and ǫ is sufficiently small, it follows
from Definition 7.1 that the normalized Ricci flow initiated from Φ∗ g(T ) exists immortally and
converges to some Einstein metric h′E nearby hE . If hE is strictly stable, then we have h′E = chE
for some positive constant c nearby 1 such that |M|dvchE = |M|dvh . Concatenating the flows in an
obvious way, we obtain the convergence of the normalized flow initiated from (M, g).             

   In Theorem 7.2, if (N, h) itself is a weakly stable Einstein manifold, then we immediately have
the following corollary.

Corollary 7.3 (Stability nearby a stable Einstein manifold). Each weakly stable Einstein man-
ifold (N, h) has a Gromov-Hausdorff neighborhood such that every Riemannian metric in this
neighborhood with Rc > −2(m − 1) can be smoothly deformed to an Einstein metric via the nor-
malized Ricci flow. Furthermore, the limit metric is homothetic to (N, h) if (N, h) is strictly stable.


    There are many cases where we can apply Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.3. For example, by
the work of Guenther-Isenberg-Knopf [50], each compact flat manifold is weakly stable. There-
fore, starting from a Riemannian manifold nearby a closed flat manifold in the Gromov-Hausdorff
topology with Rc > −2(m − 1), the normalized Ricci flow exists immortally and converges to a
flat manifold in the smooth topology. Note that the limit flat manifold may not be homothetic to
the original model flat manifold, as the flat manifold is only weakly stable. On the other hand,
each compact space form (N, h) of curvature ±1 is strictly stable. Therefore, given any nearby
metric (M, g) in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology satisfying Rc > −2(m − 1) and |M|dvg = |N|dvh ,
the Ricci flow initiated from (M, g) exists forever and converges to (N, h). For more results con-
cerning the convergence of Ricci flow nearby closed Einstein manifold, see also Šešum [88], R.G.
Ye [102], etc. A comprehensive review for the stability of Ricci flow can be found in Chapter
35 of the book [37]. The case when K = 1 is even more interesting, as we can combine Theo-
rem 7.2 with the great development in the last decade by Böhm-Wilking [7], Brendle-Schoen [9]
and Brendle [8]. In particular, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4 (Stability nearby a PIC1 -metric). Suppose (N, h) is a closed Riemannian manifold
with PIC1 curvature condition. Namely, (N, h) × (R, gE ) has positive isotropic curvature. Then
there exists a small constant ǫ = ǫ(N, h) with the following properties.
   If (M, g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying (7.3), then the normalized Ricci flow initiated
from (M, g) exists immortally and converges to a space form metric with positive curvature.


                                                   70
Proof. By the work of Brendle [8], the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (N, h) has immortal
existence and convergence. For ǫ sufficiently small, we know the normalized Ricci flow initiated
from g exists for at least time T = 1 and g̃(1) is very close to h̃(1) in C 5 -topology, via Theorem 6.6.
As PIC1 condition is an open condition preserved by the Ricci flow, we know h̃(1) and g̃(1) also
satisfy PIC1 -condition. Applying [8] again on g̃(1), we obtain the desired immortal existence and
convergence.                                                                                           

   Theorem 7.2 can also be applied to study the stability of the Kähler Ricci flows. For example,
we have the following theorem.

Theorem 7.5 (Stability nearby a Kähler Ricci flow of vanishing c1 ). Suppose (N n , h, J) is a
closed Kähler manifold of complex dimension n satisfying c1 (N, J) = 0. There exists a small
constant ǫ = ǫ(N, h, J) with the following properties.
   If (M, g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying (7.3) with m = 2n. Then the normalized Ricci
flow initiated from (M, g) exists immortally and converges to a Calabi-Yau metric (N n , h′ , J ′ ).

Proof. In light of the work of Cao [14], the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (N, h, J) con-
verges to a Calabi-Yau metric (N, hKE , J). By Bogomolov-Tian-Todorov theorem(cf. [10], [94]
and [97]), the complex structure J is integrable. Consequently, the Einstein manifold (N, hKE )
is weakly stable by Theorem 1.7 of [42]. Therefore, we conclude the immortal existence and
convergence by Theorem 7.2.                                                                   

    In all the above applications of Theorem 7.2, we a priori assume the existence of a normalized
Ricci flow solution with immortal existence and convergence. It is desirable to drop such con-
ditions and obtain existence and convergence only by initial geometric conditions like curvature,
volume, etc. Such expectation is partially achieved by the following theorem.

Theorem 7.6 (=Theorem 1.4). For each m ≥ 3, there is a constant δ0 = δ0 (m) with the following
property.
   Suppose (M m , g) is a Riemannian manifold satisfying
                                   
                                    Rc ≥ (m − 1)(1 − δ),
                                   
                                   
                                                                                                  (7.6)
                                    |M|dvg = (m + 1)ωm+1 ,
                                   

where ωm+1 is the volume of unit ball in Rm+1 , and δ ∈ (0, δ0 ). Then the normalized Ricci flow
initiated from (M m , g) exists immortally and converges to a round metric g∞ exponentially fast.
The limit metric (M, g∞ ) has constant sectional curvature 1.
   Furthermore, for each pair of points x, y ∈ M satisfying dg(0) (x, y) ≤ 1, the following distance
bi-Hölder estimate holds:
                                    1+ψ                            1−ψ
                               e−ψ dg(0) (x, y) ≤ d∞ (x, y) ≤ eψ dg(0) (x, y),                     (7.7)

where dg(0) = dg , d∞ = dg∞ , and ψ = ψ(δ|m).




                                                     71
Proof. Let ground be the space form metric on S m with constant sectional curvature 1. Clearly,

                                    dGH ((M, g), (S m , ground )) < ψ(δ|m).                                  (7.8)

Note that (S m , ground ) is a fixed point of the normalized Ricci flow. Then the immortal existence
and convergence of the normalized Ricci flow initiated from g is a consequence of Corollary 7.3.
The exponential convergence is due to Hamilton [51], [52]. Note that the flow limit metric g∞ is
isometric to ground .
    We proceed to prove (7.7). Suppose δ is small enough, by the Bishop-Gromov volume com-
parison and the Lévy-Gromov isoperimetric constant estimate(cf. [48]), one can find a fixed scale
r0 ∈ (0, 1) such that ν(M, g, r02 ) is almost non-negative such that Corollary 5.5 can be applied. It
follows that
                  1+ψ                                    1−ψ

                    e−ψ dg(ǫ 2 r2 ) (x, y) ≤ dg(1) (x, y) ≤ eψ dg(ǫ 2 r2 ) (x, y),   ∀ x, y ∈ M.            (7.10)
                               0                                        0


By exponential convergence of g(t) to g(∞) and the fact that g(1) is already very close to ground in
the smooth topology, we have

                        e−ψ dg(1) (x, y) ≤ d∞ (x, y) ≤ eψ dg(1) (x, y),           ∀ x, y ∈ M.               (7.11)

Combining (7.9), (7.10) and (7.11), up to modifying ψ if necessary, we obtain (7.7).                            

   In Riemannian geometry, the round metrics on unit sphere S m are the most well-known clas-
sical metrics of positive curvature. Their counter parts in Kähler geometry are the Fubini-Study
metrics on CPn , whose sectional curvatures vary from 1 to 4. Since the Ricci curvature of the
Fubini-Study metrics is 2(n + 1), the following theorem can be regarded as the Kähler version of
Theorem 7.6.

Theorem 7.7 (=Theorem 1.5 ). There is a constant δ0 = δ0 (n) with the following properties.
    Suppose (M n , g, J) is a Fano manifold whose metric form is proportional to c1 (M, J) and sat-
isfies                               

                                     
                                     
                                                                                            (7.12)

                                     

where Ωn is the volume of (CPn , gFS ), and δ ∈ (0, δ0 ). Then the normalized Ricci flow initiated from

such that (M, g∞ , J) is biholomorphic-isometric to (CPn , gFS , JFS ). Furthermore, for each pair of
points x, y ∈ M satisfying dg(0) (x, y) ≤ 1, the distance bi-Hölder estimate (7.7) holds.


                                                          72
Proof. By the result of Zhang [103], there exists a dimensional constant δ̄(n) such that if δ < δ̄(n),

our assumption, we have [ω] = 2πλc1 (M, J), which together with (7.12) forces that λ = 2(n + 1).
Therefore, the normalized Ricci flow

Claim 7.8. There exists δa = δa (n) with the following properties. For each (M, g) satisfies (7.12)
for some δ ∈ (0, δa ), we have

   We argue by contradiction. For otherwise, there exist δi → 0 and flows starting from gi (0) = gi
with Rc ≥ 2(n + 1)(1 − δi )gi and |M|dvgi = Ωn such that

                                                             
                                 dGH (Mi , gi ), (CPn , gFS ) ≥ ξ > 0.                          (7.19)

In light of estimate (7.17) and (7.14), we obtain a sequence of polarized Kähler Ricci flows where
Theorem 1.2 of Chen-Wang [34] applies. By taking subsequence if necessary, we have

                                         Ĉ ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                            (Mi , gi (1)) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , g∞ (1)),                     (7.20)

where the limit is a Kähler Einstein conifold in the sense of Chen-Wang(cf. Definition 1.2 of [33]).
The definition of Ĉ ∞ -Cheeger-Gromov convergence can be found on page 2 of [33]. In view of
the partial-C 0 -estimate(cf. Theorem 1.10 of [34]), we can construct a test-configuration which
achieves a degeneration from (Mi , gi (1), Ji ) to a Q-Fano Kähler-Einstein space (M∞ , g∞ (1), J∞ ).
Recall that each Mi is already biholomorphic to CPn and gi (0) corresponds to a metric form ωi (0)
in the pluri-anti-canonical class 4(n + 1)πc1 (Mi , Ji ). By the stability theorem of Chen-Donaldson-
Sun(cf. [28], [29] and [30]), we know that (M∞ , g∞ (1), J∞ ) must be biholomorphic-isometric to
the standard projective space (CPn , gFS , JFS ). In particular, the limit space (M∞ , g∞ (1), J∞ ) is
smooth. Therefore, we can apply the backward pseudo-locality theorem(cf. Theorem 4.7 of [34])
to improve the convergence (7.20) as:

In particular, dGH ((Mi , gi (1)), (CPn , gFS )) could be arbitrarily small if we choose i sufficiently
large. This contradicts our choice of gi in (7.19). The proof of Claim 7.8 is complete.
Claim 7.9. There exists δb = δb (n, ǫ0 ) with the following properties. For each (M, g) satisfies

We decompose each space, M̄ or Mi , by setting Rr = {x|vr(x) ≥ r} and Sr = {x|vr(x) < r}. Then

                               R = ∪r>0 Rr , S = ∩r>0 Sr .
For each i, as Mi is a smooth manifold, it is clear that S(Mi ) = ∅. However, S( M̄) , ∅ in general.
For simplicity of notation, we regard the m-dimensional Hausdorff measure as the default measure
on the limit space M̄, and we regard dvgi = dvgi (0) as the default measure on Mi . Then it is

                                   |Sρ ( M̄)| = | ∩r≥ρ Sr ( M̄)| < ǫ.

for large i.
   Fix ȳ ∈ R( M̄). We can assume yi ∈ Mi such that yi → ȳ along the convergence (7.23).
   Thanks to (7.16), the sequence (Mi , gi ) is an almost Einstein sequence in the sense of Tian-

By (7.26), for each small fixed t, the limit of (Mi , gi (t)) is (CPn , gFS ). Plugging (7.25) into the
above inequality and letting i → ∞, we obtain

                                        |B(ȳ, r)| ≥ |BgFS (ȳ, r)|dvgFS − ǫ.

                                                inf vr(ȳ, d̄) ≥ r0 .

Since R( M̄) is a dense subset of M̄, we can choose ȳ ∈ R( M̄) such that ȳ → x̄. Then the volume
continuity implies that vr( x̄) ≥ r0 . This contradicts (7.24). The proof of Claim 7.9 is complete.

   Thanks to Claim 7.9, we obtain
                                                vr(M, g) > 0.5r0
when (M, g) satisfies (7.12) for sufficiently small δ. In light of Theorem 5.9, the above inequality

           1+ψ                               1−ψ

The Bishop-Gromov volume comparison implies that the diameter of (M, g) is uniformly bounded.
Therefore, it follows from (7.27) that

                                     dGH {(M, g(0)), (M, g(1))} < ψ(δ|n),

which combined with (7.18) yields
                                       

Therefore, (M, g) converges in Gromov-Hausdorff topology to (CPn , gFS ) if δ → 0.

   The following corollary is the direct consequence of Theorem 7.7, which seems to be unknown
without exploiting the normalized Ricci flow.

Corollary 7.10 (Equivalence of the volume closeness and the Gromov-Hausdorff closeness).
Suppose (M n , g, J) is a Fano manifold whose metric form class is proportional to c1 (M, J) and
Rc ≥ 2(n + 1)g. Then dGH {(M, g), (CPn , gFS )} is close to 0 if and only if volume of (M, g) is close
to Ωn .

Remark 7.11. Suppose (M, J) is a Fano manifold with a Kähler Einstein metric gKE in the anti-
canonical class 2πc1 (M, J). If g is an arbitrary metric in the Kähler class 2πc1 (M, J), then
                                        Z                Z
                                   0=      (R − 2n)dv =       ∆ f dv
                                          M                    M

where f is the Ricci potential function. Therefore, if R ≥ 2n, then we must have R ≡ 2n. Along
the normalized Ricci flow initiated from g, the maximum principle on R implies that R ≥ 2n
is preserved. Then we know R ≡ 2n on the whole flow solution. Evolution equation of scalar
curvature along the flow then implies Rc − g ≡ 0. Consequently, by the uniqueness of Kähler
Einstein metrics, we obtain g = Φ∗ (gKE ) for some Φ ∈ Aut(M, J). Since Rc ≥ 1 implies R ≥ 2n,
we have the rigidity property:

                               Rc ≥ 1 ⇔ dGH {(M, g), (M, gKE )} = 0.

                                                       77
Following the proof of Theorem 7.7, one can prove a quantative version of this property:
                         Rc ≥ 1 − δ ⇔ dGH {(M, g), (M, gKE )} < ψ(δ|M, J).
We believe that Rc ≥ 1 − δ in the above inequality could be replaced by R ≥ 2n(1 − δ).

   In the remainder of this section, we shall apply the tools we developed in this and the previous
section to construct examples of immortal solutions of normalized Ricci flow. Our examples shall
show that the bi-Hölder distance distortion estimate in Corollary 5.5 is optimal. Namely, it cannot
be improved to bi-Lipschitz estimate.

   The construction above can be used to develop high dimensional examples. For simplicity, we
only point out that uniform bi-Lipschitz estimate fails along the normalized Ricci flow.

Proposition 7.13 (The failure of bi-Lipschitz equivalence). For each positive integer n and
small positive numbers ξ, ǫ, there exists a Fano manifold (M n , g, J) satisfying

                                           vr(M, g) ≥ 1,       Rcg ≥ 0,                                     (7.49)

with the following properties.
    There exists a point x0 ∈ M and ~v ∈ T p M\ {0} such that along the normalized Ricci flow
initiated from g(0) = g, we have
                                        h~v, ~vig(ξ)
                                                     < ǫ.                              (7.50)
                                        h~v, ~vig(0)
Consequently, (7.49) cannot imply uniform bi-Lipschitz equivalence between g(0) and g(ξ), no
matter how small ξ is.

Proof. Let g̃ǫ be the metric constructed above on CP1 . Then let g = g̃ǫ × gFS × · · · × gFS on the
product complex manifold M = CP1 × · · · × CP1 , which is Fano. The metric form is proportional
to the anti-canonical class. Therefore, the normalized Kähler Ricci flow initiated from g will pre-
serve the Kähler class and exist immortally. Moreover, the flow converges to the Kähler Einstein
metric gKE = gFS × gFS × · · · × gFS . Note that (M, g) satisfies (7.49) up to a rescaling of a fixed
scale. Since dGH ((M, g), (M, gKE )) → 0 as ǫ → 0, we can apply Theorem 1.3. Therefore, by the
same deduction as before, we know that gKE , g(ξ) and g(∞) are all bi-Lipschitz equivalent among
each other with Lipschitz constant 1 + ξ, whenever ǫ is small enough. However, by choosing
p = ([1 : 0], [1 : 0], · · · , [1 : 0]) and ~v ∈ T [1:0] CP1 × {0} × · · · × {0} ⊂ T p M, it follows from the
construction that
                                   h~v, ~vig(ξ)           h~v, ~vigKE        −1
                                                ≤ (1 + ξ)              < 2e−ǫ < ǫ,
                                   h~v, ~vig(0)           h~v, ~vig(0)
which is nothing but (7.50).                                                                                    

   Note that normalization in Proposition 7.13 is not important, since the logarithm of volume is
bounded along the unnormalized Ricci flow for a fixed amount of time by condition (7.49). In
the study of Riemannian manifolds of Ricci curvature bounded below, Ricci flow smoothing is
a convenient method. Proposition 7.13 indicates that there is no uniform control of the time ξ
such that the metric g(ξ) and g(0) are uniformly bi-Lipschitz equivalent, even if we have the extra
Kähler condition. Since Kähler geometry has independent interest, it is desirable to find conditions
to guarantee the lower bound of ξ along the Kähler Ricci flow. It turns out that scalar curvature
being locally bounded is a sufficient condition, which will be studied in the next section.

                                                         82
8 The pseudo-locality theorem in the Kähler Ricci flow

A Kähler manifold (M n , g, J) is a Riemannian manifold with real dimension m = 2n and an almost
complex structure J which is parallel under the Levi-Civita connection, i.e. ∇g J ≡ 0. If we run the
Ricci flow starting from a closed Kähler manifold (M n , g, J), then it is well known that the Kähler
condition is preserved by the flow. Namely, for each t, we have ∇g(t) J ≡ 0. The Kähler Ricci
flow admits some amazing rigidities. For example, the identity map between different time slices
of a Kähler Ricci flow is automatically biholomorphic and therefore harmonic. In this section,
we shall take advantage of the special properties of the Kähler Ricci flow to further improve the
pseudo-locality theorems.
    This section is structured as follows. First we show a key improvement from the Riemannian
Ricci flow: the metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence. Roughly speaking, when the initial metric is
locally almost Euclidean, then near the central part along the flow, all g(·, t) are quasi-isometric to
g(·, 0). Note that here almost Euclidean condition also requires scalar curvature being bounded.
This bi-Lipschitz equivalence is achieved by Theorem 8.1 and Corollary 8.5. Then we construct
local holomorphic charts and study the Kähler Ricci flow in the local charts. It turns out that the
localization in the Kähler Ricci flow is much more straightforward than the one in the Riemannian
Ricci flow. Besides −R, we have another quantity (R + |∇ϕ̇|2 ) as a local sub-heat-solution. Here
ϕ̇ is local Ricci potential function, which can be adjusted by adding linear functions. Exploiting
the local maximum principle for them, we obtain the growth estimates for the scalar curvature
bounds in Proposition 8.6. Combining the scalar curvature bounds and the metric bi-Lipschitz
equivalence, we then deduce C 1,α -estimate on the metrics via complex Monge-Ampère equations.
This is done in Theorem 8.8. As applications, in Theorem 8.9, we show a C 1,α -compactness
theorem for Kähler manifolds whose scalar curvatures are uniformly bounded and whose entropy
radii are uniformly bounded from below. If the limit is a smooth Kähler manifold, we also obtain
the lower and upper bound of scalar curvatures being preserved under the C 1,α -convergence. In
Corollary 8.10, we show that the assumption of the smooth Kähler manifold limit is natural under
some circumstances. Finally, we finish this section by proving Theorem 1.7 and Theorem 1.8.

Theorem 8.1. (Metric bi-Lipschitz equivalence at the center point) For each small constant ξ,
there exists ǫ = ǫ(n, ξ) with the following property.

   Up to now, the scale L is fixed. In (8.10) and (8.15), the definitions of U and V depends on

ǫ → 0. Note that we can always fix L first and then let L → ∞. Then we shall use the estimates de-

   From previous steps, we already know that gϕ0 −−−−→ gE and gϕ1 −−−−→ gE . Therefore, gϕ0 (0)

    The scalar curvature bounds in Proposition 8.6 can be used to improve the metric bi-Lipschitz
estimates to C 1,α -estimates. The key input is the application of the theory of complex Monge-
Ampère equation.
Theorem 8.8. (Metric C 1,α -equivalence near the center point) For each α ∈ (0, 1) and Q > 1,
there is a constant ǭ = ǭ(n, α, Q) with the following property.
   Suppose {(M n , g(t), J), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1} is a Ricci flow solution, x0 ∈ M and r ∈ (0, 1). Suppose (8.1)
hold for ǫ ∈ (0, ǭ), then there is a biholomorphic map Φ : Bg(0) (x0 , ǫr) → U ⊂ Cn such that

                             B(0, Q−1 ) ⊂ U ⊂ B(0, Q);                                         (8.74)
                                  −2                                 2 2
                              (ǫr) Φ∗ g(t) C1,α (U) < Q, ∀ t ∈ [0, ǫ r ].                      (8.75)



                                                  96
Proof. This theorem basically follows from the combination of Theorem 8.1 and Proposition 8.6.
Since no new ingredients will be needed in the proof, we shall be sketchy. We choose the bi-
holomorphic map Φ as done nearby (8.13) in the proof of Theorem 8.1, with 21 being replaced by
any α ∈ (0, 1) and L = 100. By (8.48), (8.51) and (8.56), we see that the constant C in (8.13)
could be replaced by an arbitrary number Q > 1, whenever ǫ is sufficiently small. Therefore
(8.74) is proved. It follows from Proposition 8.6 that the scalar curvature is uniformly bounded on
B(x0 , 2ǫr) × [0, (ǫr)2 ]. Following the argument in Claim 8.3 and in the proof of (8.56), we obtain

Theorem 8.9 (Compactness of moduli of Kähler manifolds with proper bounds). Suppose
(Min , gi , Ji ) is a sequence of Kähler manifolds of complex dimension n satisfying

                              er(M, g) ≥ ι,           diam(M, g) ≤ D,         σa ≤ R ≤ σb .                       (8.76)

Here er = erǫ0 is the entropy radius defined in Definition 5.10 for some sufficiently small ǫ0 =
ǫ0 (n). For each α ∈ (0, 1), by taking subsequence if necessary, we have

                                                  C 1,α −Cheeger−Gromov
                                (Mi , gi , Ji ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) ,                             (8.77)

where (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a C 1,α -Kähler manifold. Furthermore, if we assume (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a
smooth Kähler manifold, then it also satisfies estimate (8.76).

Proof. For each t0 sufficiently small(determined by n and ι in (8.76)), it follows from the geometric
estimates (1.5), (1.6) in the pseudo-locality theorem and Shi’s estimates of curvature derivatives
that
                                                   C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                     (Mi , gi (t0 ), Ji ) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (M∞ , g∞ (t0 ), J∞ ) .
                  ∞

                                                      C∞                         C∞
                                      ϕ∗i (gi (t0 )) → g∞ (t0 ),       ϕ∗i (Ji ) → J∞ .                           (8.78)

          ϕ∗i (gi ) − ϕ∗i (gi (t0 )) C1,α (ϕ∗ (g (t ))) = ϕ∗i (gi (0)) − ϕ∗i (gi (t0 )) C1,α (ϕ∗ (g (t ))) < C.   (8.79)

In particular, we obtain (8.77).
    If we further assume that (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a smooth Kähler manifold, then we can run the
Kähler Ricci flow starting from (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ). The flow exists for a definite time period [0, δ0 ]. It
is important to observe that {(M∞ , g∞ (t), J∞ ), 0 ≤ t ≤ δ0 } is a smooth space-time. Fix each point
x ∈ M∞ and t ∈ (0, δ0 ], the commutativity of Gromov-Hausdorff convergence and the Ricci flow
implies that
                                   1                                      1
                           σa − Ct 5 ≤ R(x, t) = lim R(xi , t) ≤ σb + Ct 5 ,
                                                   i→∞

where xi ∈ Mi and xi → x as (Mi , gi (t)) converges to (M∞ , g∞ (t)). Then we let t → 0+ in the
above inequality and obtain σa ≤ R(x, 0) ≤ σb . By the arbitrary choice of x, we arrive at the scalar
curvature bound of (M∞ , g∞ ) in (8.76). The entropy radius lower bound follows from the C 1,α -
convergence and Lemma 2.9. The diameter upper bound is obvious. Therefore, all the estimates
in (8.76) hold for (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ).                                                                

   In Theorem 8.9, the assumption that (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a smooth Kähler manifold seems strong.
The following corollary shows that if the scalar curvatures oscillate in magnitudes tending to zero,
then the limit is automatically a smooth Kähler manifold.

Corollary 8.10 (Smooth cscK limit spaces). Same conditions as in Theorem 8.9. If we further
assume K − δi ≤ R Mi ≤ K + δi for δi → 0, then (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a smooth Kähler manifold whose
scalar curvature equals K everywhere.

Proof. Fix each point x ∈ M∞ , we can regard it as the limit point of xi ∈ Mi . Therefore, by
assumption and Theorem 8.8, we can find uniform C 1,α -holomorphic chart near xi . In the holo-
morphic chart, we have
                                    −∆ log det gβγ̄ = R.
Since kR − KkC0 (Mi ) → 0, in the holomorphic coordinate chart around x, the metric tensor gβγ̄ has
uniform C 1,α estimate and satisfies the equation

                                        −∆ log det gβγ̄ − K = 0

in the distribution sense. Then standard bootstrapping argument implies that gβγ̄ is a smooth metric
tensor with constant scalar curvature K nearby x. By the arbitrary choice of x ∈ M∞ , we know
that (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) is a smooth Kähler manifold with constant scalar curvature K.               

    In view of Lemma 2.9, the entropy radius is bounded from below by the isoperimetric ra-
dius(cf. Remark 5.11), up to multiplying a definite constant. Therefore, Theorem 8.9 and Corol-
lary 8.10 can be regarded as generalizations of Theorem 3.9, replacing cscK manifolds by Kähler
manifolds with bounded scalar curvature.
   We close this section by the proof of Theorem 1.7 and Theorem 1.8. Note that Theorem 1.8
provides another condition to guarantee that the limit (M∞ , g∞ , J∞ ) in Theorem 8.9 is a smooth
Kähler manifold.

Proof of Theorem 1.7: It follows from the combination of Proposition 8.6 and Theorem 8.8.              


                                                   98
Proof of Theorem 1.8: The preservation of both upper and lower bounds of scalar curvature were

Kähler structure compatible with the smooth metric g.
   In light of Lemma 2.9, the C 0 -approximation condition (1.24) implies that entropy radius is
bounded from below. Therefore, (8.76) holds and we can apply the pseudo-locality theorem.
Following the proof of Theorem 6.1, via the pseudo-locality theorem and the Ricci-DeTurck flow,
we obtain the commutativity of taking C 0 -limit and running the Ricci flow. For simplicity of
notation, we denote (Min , fi∗ gi , fi∗ Ji ) by (Min , gi , Ji ). Then we can rewrite (1.24) as

                                                        C0
                                                   gi → g.                                   (8.81)

For each small t fixed, we have
                                             C ∞ −Cheeger−Gromov
                              (Mi , gi (t)) −−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ (N, g(t)),
where g(t) is the time-t-slice of the Ricci flow initiated from g. Fix t0 sufficiently small and set
         √

                         Bhi (p, 2r) = Bϕ∗i (gi (0)) (p, 2r) ⊂ Bϕ∗i (gi (t0 )) (p, 10r).

                                                             

a smooth metric tensor on U. Abusing notation again, we may denote Φ∗ (g) by g. Then g is a
smooth metric tensor in the coordinate U.

   Since (8.85) holds in each Uβ , following our conventions of notation we have

                                                       C 1,α

which combined with (8.81) implies that (N, g) and (N, h) are isometric to each other. Therefore,
there exists an isometry F : (N, g) → (N, h). Namely, for each pair of points x, y ∈ N, we have

                                        dg (x, y) = dh (F(x), F(y)).

                                                       100
    For each p ∈ N, as discussed above, we can choose r small enough and find V ⊂ Cn such that
(V, h) is isometric to a geodesic ball (Bh (F(p), r), h) and

                                                    Id
                                             (V, h) −−→ (V, gE )                                      (8.89)

is a harmonic map, and h is a C 1,α -metric tensor on V. Also, we can find U ⊂ Cn such that (U, g)
is isometric to the geodesic ball (Bg (p, r), g) such that g is a smooth metric tensor on U. By the
definition of F, it is clear that F|(Bg (p,r),g) is an isometry map from (Bg (p, r), g) to (Bh (F(p), r), h).
Consequently, abusing notation again, we have an isometry
                                                     F
                                             (U, g) −−
                                                     → (V, h).                                        (8.90)

Notice that both U and V are open sets in Euclidean space. Now g is a C ∞ -metric tensor field,
while h is only a C 1,α -metric tensor field. The isometry property of F clearly implies that F is a bi-
Lipschitz map. The harmonicity in (8.89) and the isometry in (8.90) together then imply that each
coordinate of F is a continuous harmonic function(cf. Section 2 of [93]). Let F = (y1 , y2 , · · · , y2n )
with each yk = yk (x1 , x2 , · · · , x2n ). The harmonicity then implies that
                                             (       2
                                                                               )
                                       k       ij ∂         1 ∂  √ i j ∂
                          0 = ∆g y = g                    − √       gg           yk
                                                 ∂xi ∂x j     g ∂xi       ∂x j

for each k ∈ {1, 2, · · · , 2n}, in the distribution sense. Since g is a smooth metric tensor, standard
regularity theory of elliptic PDE then implies that each yk is a smooth function. Therefore, F is
a smooth map from U to V. Consequently, the isometry property in (8.90) forces h = F∗ g to be
smooth
n o metric tensor field on V. According to the choice of V, it is clear that V is compatible with
 Uβ . This means that h is a smooth metric tensor around point F(p). By the arbitrary choice
     β∈I                                                                      n o
of p ∈ N, we know that h is a smooth metric tensor field with respect to Uβ . Combining this
                                                                                  β∈I
smoothness with (8.87) and (8.88), we know that (N, h, J) is a smooth Kähler manifold. By the
arbitrary choice of p and the local smoothness of isometry in (8.90), we know that the isometry
F : (N, g) → (N, h) is a smooth map so that h = F∗ (g). This means that (N, g, F ∗ (J)) is a smooth
Kähler manifold. Replacing F ∗ (J) by J if necessary, we obtain the desired smooth Kähler structure
compatible with g. The proof of Theorem 1.8 is complete.                                              


9 Further Questions

In our stability theorems, we only prove the existence of small gap constants. It seems very natural
to ask the following question.

Question 9.1. Explicitly calculate the sharp gap constant in Theorem 1.4, Theorem 1.5 and The-
orem 1.6. In the particular case m = 4, we conjecture the constant in Theorem 1.4 is 14 , which
represents the gap of volume ratio between (S 4 , ground ) and (CP2 , gFS ).

   More boldly, we may even drop the initial Ricci curvature condition, or replace it with scalar
curvature lower bound condition at least. Recall that the main theme of [99] and the current paper


                                                    101
is: under Ricci flow, the local entropy functionals µ and ν are more natural than the volume.
Although the Ricci curvature lower bound and the volume lower bound are currently needed for
many technical purposes, we believe that finally they can be essentially replaced by a lower bound
condition concerning only the local functional µ or ν. Therefore, the main theorems in this paper
should have versions where only initial entropy conditions are assumed. Inspired by the recent
development in the mean curvature flow through a series of work of Colding-Ilmanen-Minicozzi-
White [41], Bernstein-Wang [4], Ketover-Zhou [69] and J.J. Zhu [105], we make the following
conjectures.
Conjecture 9.2. Among all Fano manifolds (M n , g, J) such that [ω] is proportional to 2πc1 (M, J),
we have
                                 ν(M, g) ≤ ν(CPn , gFS ).
The equality holds if and only if (M, g, J) is biholomorphic-isometric to (CPn , gFS , JFS ). Further-
more, there exists a constant ǫ = ǫ(n) such that if

                                     ν(M, g) > ν(CPn , gFS ) − ǫ,                                 (9.1)

then (M, J) is biholomorphic to (CPn , JFS ) and the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (M, g) will
converge exponentially fast to a metric with constant positive holomorphic sectional curvature.
Conjecture 9.3. Among all closed Riemannian manifold (M, g) of real dimension m, we have

                                      ν(M, g) ≤ ν(S m , ground ).

The equality holds if and only if M is isometric to the round sphere (S m , ground ). Furthermore,
there exists a constant ǫ = ǫ(m) such that if

                                    ν(M, g) > ν(S m , ground ) − ǫ,                               (9.2)

then M is diffeomorphic to S m and the normalized Ricci flow initiated from (M, g) will converge
exponentially fast to a round sphere.

   Similar to Question 9.1, it is intriguing to sharply calculate the value of ǫ in (9.1) and (9.2).
